NR-03 is a large, one-way step — a new vendored dependency and a different float library — and taking it on a prediction would repeat the pattern D-92 was decided on. The shims are required by NR-04 regardless of which library wins, so nothing done here is wasted. Twice in this session a measurement has overturned a prediction, once my own: the first operation matrix reported MUL and constant SHL as wrong and both proved correct once the probe's own scaffolding was fixed |
| D-105 | **NR-03 is invoked. Berkeley SoftFloat Release 2c is vendored as the soft backend for MVS.** Approved download: `SoftFloat-2c.zip`, 108,086 bytes, from `www.jhauser.us/arithmetic/` — the same author and canonical site D-28 took Release 3e from. It is committed under `third_party/` like 3e | Try a newer GCCMVS first, now that there are four specific defects to test it against; make JCC the primary compiler (SRS text changes to D-03 and the Gate G1 exit criterion); supply ONFLY-owned `@@UCMPDI`, `@@UDIVDI` and `@@UMODDI` and keep 3e | Gate G1 is answered and the answer is comprehensive: GCCMVS fails on 64-bit integers in **four independent ways** — internal compiler errors at 3590, 447, 902 and 6970; a silent wrong answer, the variable-count left shift zeroing bit 63 (VL-19); no runtime helpers in PDPCLIB, with `@@UCMPDI`, `@@UDIVDI` and `@@UMODDI` all unresolved (VL-21); and a code-generation bug asking for them through `=A()` where an external routine needs `=V()`. 2c implements binary64 in 32-bit integers only, which removes all four at once rather than one at a time — there is no 64-bit compare, divide or modulo to call, and no variable 64-bit shift to be wrong. Supplying the helpers was rejected on the measurement: it fixes one defect of four, leaving `s_shortShiftRightJam64.c` ICEing and the shift silently wrong. This is NR-03 and D-06 operating exactly as written, on measurement rather than prediction (D-104) |
| D-106 | **The SoftFloat 2c integration plan is approved.** In order: vendor 2c verbatim and record its provenance and licence in `third_party/MANIFEST.md`; write ONFLY's 2c configuration under `softfloat/c2c/` — a processor header **without** `BITS64`, plus `milieu.h`, `softfloat.h` and `softfloat-specialize` derived from upstream's templates by the substitutions upstream prescribes; then build 2c's `bits32` on x86 and **prove or disprove bit-identity with 3e** using TT-02's TestFloat vectors and `tools/cmpback.py`. The `onf_fp` backend and the MVS build come only after that result | Change the shape or ordering, for example proving 2c on MVS first or writing the backend before the identity check; discuss the approach and the risk to ACC-5 first | The identity check is the result that decides whether this swap is cheap or expensive, so it is front-loaded. Every ACC-5 fingerprint in the golden suite was computed with 3e; if 2c and 3e disagree on any of ONFLY's six binary64 operations, every fingerprint changes and that is a far larger decision than choosing a library. Building the backend first would mean discovering that after the expensive part rather than before it. 2c's `bits32` `float64` is a two-word struct rather than a scalar, so the backend is real work and is deliberately not started on an unproven premise |
| D-107 | **ONFLY's 2c build uses the ARM-VFPv2 default-NaN convention, matching D-31.** The `softfloat-specialize` file is written to that convention rather than taken from upstream's shipped 386 configuration | Use 2c's shipped 386-Win32-GCC conventions as-is; defer the choice, since VL-11 already excludes NaN cases from TT-02 and FR-SIM-08 aborts on NaN rather than propagating it | D-31 is an owner decision already on record and nothing has been measured that disturbs it. Keeping it means the 3e and 2c backends differ in as few respects as possible, so any disagreement the D-106 identity check finds is attributable to the arithmetic rather than to NaN policy — a mismatch under the shipped 386 conventions would have needed a second investigation to separate the two causes. The cost is a hand-written specialize file instead of a shipped one, which is small and follows D-35's precedent for ONFLY-owned SoftFloat pieces |
| D-108 | **The MVS build of SoftFloat 2c comes before the `onf_fp` backend.** D-106 left both "after the identity check" without an order between them | Write the `onf_fp` backend first and prove the golden fingerprints under 2c on x86; do both in one slice without asking again; stop and report after the 2c integration | The whole case for D-105 is that 2c avoids the four defects GCCMVS has, and that had never been tested on GCCMVS. The tooling already exists — `tools/mvsbld.py` builds the deck and 2c's `bits32` is a single translation unit — so it is roughly one job, while the backend is substantial work that a GCCMVS failure would strand. Ordering the cheap decisive measurement ahead of the expensive dependent work is the same reasoning as D-97, D-102 and D-106 |
| D-109 | **Before amalgamating, probe GCCMVS for an include mechanism that bypasses 8-character member naming.** A job or two: what options the compiler actually accepts, and whether any of them — GCC 3.x's `-remap`, an `-I` form naming a DD or dataset, or anything else it lists — changes include resolution | Generate an MVS-only amalgamation now (which would need D-94's reasoning explicitly set aside); reconsider the compiler instead, reopening what D-105 settled; write the `onf_fp` backend first and leave VL-23 recorded | VL-23 measures the member rule but not the full option space, and GCC 3.x does ship `-remap` precisely for filesystems that permit only very short names, which is this situation exactly. If such a mechanism exists the amalgamation is unnecessary and D-94's reasoning stands untouched; if it does not, the amalgamation is then taken on a measured absence rather than on an assumption that nothing else was available. The cost is one or two jobs against a lab that is already running |
| D-110 | **SoftFloat 2c reaches GCCMVS as a generated amalgamation, built for MVS only.** A tool inlines 2c's quoted includes in order, recursively, from the unmodified vendored and generated files, and the result is assembled **at submit time and never written to disk**, so there is no second copy of the source to drift from the first. The include-name to file mapping is stated by the caller, never derived. **D-94's reasoning is explicitly set aside for this case** | Reconsider the compiler instead, reopening D-96's options; write the `onf_fp` backend first and leave the blocker recorded; stop and report | VL-23 and VL-24 together measure that there is no alternative: GCCMVS resolves an include to the member named by the first eight characters, 2c's `softfloat.h`, `softfloat-macros` and `softfloat-specialize` therefore name one member, and the compiler's own option list — read from `--help` rather than guessed — contains nothing that changes it, `-remap` included, which was tested and is inert. D-94 rejected amalgamation for TT-01 because it would introduce a build-time transformation of **the engine source**, which a reviewer could fairly read as a second form of it and so as a strain on NFR-PRT-01. Two things differ here: this is a vendored third-party library rather than ONFLY's engine, and it is forced by a measured compiler limit rather than chosen to avoid arranging members. Generating in memory rather than into a file is what keeps D-94's actual worry — two forms of one source — from arising at all |
| D-111 | **SoftFloat 2c's external names are renamed by a generated `#define` prologue, and the C-04 lint is extended to enforce on vendored externals rather than merely report them.** The renames are additive, so `third_party` stays byte-identical, and they are applied on x86 as well as MVS so the mechanism is exercised by the ordinary suite rather than only by a lab job | Do the renames without touching the lint; reconsider the compiler instead; stop and report the finding | VL-25 measures 38 of 2c's 42 externals colliding at eight characters, and splitting the library across translation units would only move the collision from the assembler to the linkage editor. The renames make the build C-04 compliant by construction instead of by exemption. Extending the lint is what stops this recurring: it would have caught the collision before a job was ever submitted, and it catches the same latent problem in Release 3e, whose `softfloat_*` names the lint already reports and does not enforce. The accepted cost is that MVS symbol names no longer match the source names, which a later link map or debugger will show, and that the lint will fail on 3e until 3e's own status is settled |
| D-112 | **TT-02 is run on MVS with a TestFloat vector table shipped from x86.** `testfloat_gen` cannot run on MVS, so the vectors are generated on x86 and transported the way `generated/onf2cv.h` already is, then the six operations are run under GCCMVS | Re-scope TT-01 first, since 2c removes its premise; write the `onf_fp` backend for 2c; stop and report on VL-26 | It closes half of Gate G1's exit criterion properly and reuses everything this session built — the transport, the amalgamation, the renames and a driver already proven to link and run on MVS. The honest cost is that a shipped table is a sample rather than the live 260,376-case stream, so the MVS run covers fewer cases than x86 does; how many is a size-against-coverage choice to be brought back |
| D-113 | **Release 3e's externals are renamed too, so the C-04 lint can enforce everywhere with no exemption.** | Leave 3e report-only, since NR-03 makes 2c the MVS backend and 3e is not on a path to an MVS linkage editor; record it as a TBC in Appendix B and settle it when z/OS is reached | Removes a latent problem rather than carrying it, and makes the lint's rule uniform instead of conditional on a judgement about which objects will one day be linked where. The accepted cost is touching the backend every x86 result so far was produced with. **This decision could not be executed as stated — see VL-27** |
| D-114 | **D-113 is amended in the light of VL-27: the eight safe names are renamed by `#define`, and for the five that are upstream feature-test macros ONFLY supplies the implementation under a compliant name, derived mechanically from upstream's own body.** The macro is then used as upstream designed it — declaring that the platform provides the primitive — rather than fighting it | Rename the eight and carry a named lint exemption for the five; revert to leaving 3e report-only; defer 3e entirely and spend the session on D-112's TT-02 | VL-27 measured that a `#define` on those five does not rename them but **deletes** them, taking upstream's declaration and definition together. Using the extension point as intended turns that from a hazard into the supported mechanism, and D-35 is the precedent: ONFLY already owns a hand-derived `roundPackToF64` and a mechanically derived `subMagsF64` for the same class of reason. The result is full C-04 compliance with no exemption anywhere, which is what makes the lint's rule uniform. The accepted cost is that ONFLY now owns five more float primitives, each of which has to be proven bit-exact — the golden suite and TT-02 are what prove it, and until they pass the change is not done |
| D-115 | **The TT-02 table size for MVS is measured, not chosen: increasing sizes are submitted until MVS refuses, and the largest that passes is used and its limit recorded.** | 12,000 vectors; 2,000 vectors; 48,000 vectors | Every size on offer was an estimate resting on one data point — a 7,741-card deck taking about twelve seconds — and on a guess about whether a large static table fits an 8M region. The project has now twice had an estimate overturned by a measurement, once my own. A measured limit is also worth more than the number itself: it is the figure Gate G3's performance work and any future MVS table will be sized against, and it is recorded once rather than re-guessed |
| D-116 | **TBD-14 is closed at 8M usable.** The Appendix B row records the measurement; C-01's and NFR-MEM-01's text is left as written, since closing the item it points at is not a change to either requirement | Close it at 16M, which TK5 also accepts, giving NFR-MEM-01 twice the headroom; keep TBD-14 open until a real ONFLY network has been loaded on MVS | The measurement is unambiguous and was taken one job per value: 8M accepted, 9M through 12M rejected with `ABEND S822`, 16M accepted. 16M was rejected as the answer because it is the whole 24-bit address space including the nucleus and every system area — no job can use all of it, and sizing a network against it would overstate what fits. The caveat is closed with the item rather than left implicit: 8M is what a job may **request**, and a compile in this session hit its own `S878` ceiling well below it with `REGION=16M` in force, so the figure a running program can obtain is smaller and still unmeasured (VL-29) |
| D-117 | **TT-01 is re-scoped and then run, as the remaining half of Gate G1.** | Write the `onf_fp` backend for 2c first; stop and report on VL-26 and VL-29 | TT-02 now passes under GCCMVS (VL-29) and TT-01 is the only other thing Gate G1's exit criterion names. Its premise has gone: TT-01 is a 64-bit integer self-test and, under NR-03 and D-105, the MVS path uses no 64-bit integers at all — the test cannot be compiled there, since `onfirun` ICEs (VL-21), and VL-19 says it would fail if it could. Settling what TT-01 means now is therefore the only thing standing between here and a closeable Gate G1, and the backend, while larger, closes no part of the stated criterion |
| D-118 | **TT-01 keeps its 64-bit form where the type exists, and gains a 32-bit form (TT-01/32) for platforms whose engine uses none. NR-14's text and Gate G1's exit criterion are amended accordingly (authorised SRS text changes)** | Rewrite TT-01 over 32-bit halves and drop the 64-bit form everywhere; narrow NR-14 so TT-01 is simply not applicable where no 64-bit integer is used, leaving Gate G1 on MVS resting on TT-02 alone | NR-14 exists for risk R-01 — a compiler synthesising wrong integer arithmetic — and its value comes from testing the arithmetic that actually runs. Under NR-03 the MVS path uses no 64-bit integers, so the existing TT-01 there tests something the engine never does, cannot be compiled (`onfirun` ICEs, VL-21) and would fail if it could (VL-19). But SoftFloat 2c's 32-bit arithmetic on MVS currently has no L0 check at all, and this compiler has already been measured returning a silently wrong answer once, so simply declaring TT-01 inapplicable would leave the least trustworthy toolchain the least tested. Dropping the 64-bit form was rejected because 3e and its 64-bit types remain the backend on x86 and on the s390x and z/OS paths |
| D-119 | **Gate G1 is recorded CLOSED, with Spike S1's own answer noted separately.** The Section 9.1 row cites VL-29 and VL-30 and states plainly that Spike S1 as originally framed — "can GCCMVS build and run SoftFloat 3e correctly?" — was answered **NO**, and that what passes is Release 2c under NR-03's fallback | Close the gate without distinguishing the two; leave Gate G1 open until ONFLY engine code itself compiles and runs on MVS | Both halves of the criterion as D-118 amended it have run on real MVS 3.8j under GCCMVS and passed. Noting S1 separately keeps the record from reading better than it is: the gate is satisfied by the fallback the SRS planned for, not by the primary path, and a reader who saw only "G1 closed" would take away that 3e works on MVS, which VL-21 measured to be false. Holding the gate open for engine code was rejected as stricter than its own stated terms — that is Phase E's work, and a gate that cannot close on its criterion is not a gate |
| D-120 | **The session finishes on the GOAL REPORT.** The `docs/implementations` note covering the 2c integration, C-04, TT-02 and TT-01/32 on MVS is written first, since the project's standing convention is that no session ends with undocumented changes and much of this currently lives only in the SRS and in commit messages | Carry on into the `onf_fp` backend for 2c | Gate G1 was the session's subject and its criterion is met, so this is a completed front rather than an interrupted one. The backend opens a large new piece of work that would be better begun cold |
| D-121 | **Session scope 2026-09-11 (second session of the day): the `onf_fp` backend over SoftFloat 2c, and then the first ONFLY engine build on MVS.** Chosen by the owner over the narrower x86-only backend slice | The 2c backend on x86 only, ending on a closed front; Gate G4, the COBOL spike (S4); Gate G2, the transport spike (S2) | The owner selected the larger scope knowing, as stated in the question, that the MVS half is where new GCCMVS defects would surface and that the session may therefore end mid-discovery rather than on a finished front. The x86 half is a precondition for the MVS half, so the narrower option is contained inside this one |
| D-122 | **The plan for D-121's scope is approved as written.** Stage 1 on x86: an `onf_fp` backend over SoftFloat 2c, TU-02 and TT-02 run through that backend, and the golden suite's thirteen fingerprints compared byte-for-byte against the Python oracle, the 3e soft backend and the native backend; then Stage 2 on MVS: ONFLY's own units compiled under GCCMVS against x86-generated known answers, then the decoder and kernel against a small synthetic network embedded as a C array | Approve Stage 1 and re-ask before any MVS job; approve but drop the synthetic-network step; discuss the shape first | The owner approved the whole plan knowing, as stated in the question, that Stage 2 may run out of road and that a real ONFNET dataset cannot be used because an 885 KB binary needs Gate G2 transport, which is not done |
| D-123 | **SoftFloat 2c's global state is removed by derivation, exactly as D-34 and D-35 did for Release 3e.** `softfloat/derive2c.py` is extended to emit a modified `softfloat.c` in which the `float_exception_flags` writes are removed and the `float_rounding_mode` and `float_detect_tininess` reads are replaced by the constants NR-01 and NR-10 already fix. VL-22's TestFloat identity check is re-run to prove the results did not change | Accept the globals for the MVP and carry NFR-MNT-02 compliance as an open item due before Phase G; measure the derivation cost first and decide afterwards | Measured on 2026-09-11, not assumed: `third_party/SoftFloat-2c/softfloat/bits32/softfloat.c:187` writes `float_exception_flags` inside `roundAndPackFloat64`, and lines 386, 1337 and 1668 read `float_detect_tininess` and `float_rounding_mode` — all on the binary64 add, subtract and multiply path ONFLY uses. Leaving them would give the MVS backend writable static data that the 3e backend does not have, which NFR-MNT-02 forbids and which the future CICS path (Section 3.7) cannot tolerate |
| D-124 | **The float backend identifier gains a third value: the manifest prints `SOFT3E`, `SOFT2C` or `NATIVE`. NFR-OBS-01's text and the Section 8.3 determinism matrix are amended accordingly (authorised SRS text changes)** | Keep `SOFT`/`NATIVE` as NFR-OBS-01 states and add a separate field carrying the release; keep `SOFT` alone and carry the ambiguity as a caveat | Two different soft libraries can now produce golden fingerprints, and a determinism claim that cannot name the library that produced it is not a claim. One unambiguous field is cheaper to read than a field plus a qualifier, and every report this project writes has to state its backend anyway |
| D-125 | **INTERCOMM is investigated on TK5 before any phase plan changes.** The `INT.JCL.CNTL` setup jobs are run, INTERCOMM is brought up, and one trivial transaction is driven from the console. Only then is it decided whether Phase G is re-scoped. **This authorises installing INTERCOMM into the running lab**, which modifies the master catalog, `SYS2.PROCLIB` and `SYS1.VTAMLST` | Re-scope Phase G now and demonstrate on TK5; record the finding and leave D-24's ordering alone; settle the noncommercial-clause question first | Measured, not assumed: TK5 carries **no CICS at all** — 719 datasets across its volumes, none of them CICS — and CICS/OS-VS was never released on MVS 3.8j's terms, so Phase G as literally written can never run in this lab. But volume `INT001` is attached at device 0380 and holds a complete, licensed **INTERCOMM 9.0**, which upstream's own README calls "a competitor of CICS". D-23's actual goal is "the fly as a transaction", and that is reachable here. Committing the SRS to it before INTERCOMM has been seen to start would repeat the pattern D-92 was decided on; a working transaction on the owner's own machine is also exactly what D-01 says the permissions request should be built from. **Open and deliberately not settled here:** an ONFLY transaction would use INTERCOMM's macros and copybooks, and whether that makes it a derivative work under the licence's noncommercial-only clause bears on NFR-LIC-01 and D-62 |
| D-126 | **TBD-15 is reopened and the phase ordering changed: the transaction work moves ahead of z/OS.** The order becomes A, B, C, D, E, **G**, F, H — Phase G is re-scoped from "CICS" into a **second MVP**, a transaction demonstrator on the owner's own TK5 under INTERCOMM, and CICS on z/OS becomes a new Phase H. The permissions request is made with Phase G in hand, not Phase E alone | Keep D-24's ordering and record INTERCOMM only as the answer to TBD-13; settle the licence question before changing anything; link a trivial ONFLY subsystem first and decide after that | D-24's ordering put CICS last for two reasons, and D-125 measured that only one of them is real. The technical dependency — a transaction cannot LINK to an engine that does not build on the platform — still holds for **CICS on z/OS**, which is why Phase H stays behind F. The environmental one does not: TK5 has no CICS and never can (D-125), but it has a licensed, installed INTERCOMM that starts, routes transactions and closes down cleanly, so "the fly as a transaction" — D-23's actual words — is reachable in the owner's lab **without z/OS at all**. D-01 says the permissions request is made with a working MVP in hand; a reviewer can be shown a batch job, or shown a transaction they can watch, and the second is the stronger argument. Phase E is deliberately not grown to absorb this: the MVP is finished without it |
| D-127 | **The Phase G demonstrator uses both surfaces: the transaction flow on a real 3270, and the live view of the network rendered on x86 from the responses** | A 3270 screen alone, keeping the whole demonstration on the mainframe; an x86 viewer alone, reading responses, with no 3270 at all | The two halves answer different objections. A 3270 screen is what makes the transaction unmistakably a mainframe transaction — an x86 window alone invites the reply that a PC drew the picture. But a 3270 is a poor surface for rendering a network, and the brain is the thing worth looking at. Taking both costs the most work and needs TBD-17 closed, since this host has no 3270 client at all and every MVS interaction so far has gone through the card reader, the printer and the Hercules console |
| D-128 | **"Live" means the engine streams intermediate state during a single run**, not one request per frame and not a recorded trace replayed afterwards. This requires a new engine output path and is recorded as TBD-19 | One request per frame, refreshing the view per response, which needs no engine change at all; record a trace on the mainframe and replay it smoothly afterwards | It is the only one of the three where the audience watches the simulation happen rather than watching its results arrive. The cost is real and is stated rather than discovered later: the engine today returns spike counts and first-spike latencies **at the end of a run**, so this adds an output path that FR-SIM and the IR-COM record layouts do not have, and it collides directly with the performance question Gate G3 has never measured — an estimated two to three orders of magnitude slower than x86 under Hercules means a streamed run may need a much smaller network or a much shorter duration than the golden fixture uses. TBD-19 carries the interface design; the estimate is not a measurement and G3 must produce one before Phase G is planned in detail |
| D-129 | **Raincode's free edition is verified before the transaction plan commits to it.** Whether it actually *runs* CICS transactions, or only compiles the syntax, decides between building ONFLY's transaction layer on Raincode, on INTERCOMM, on both, or on neither | Use Raincode for the API and INTERCOMM for the hardware; use Raincode alone and drop INTERCOMM; use INTERCOMM alone and ignore Raincode | The owner found the Raincode suggestion in an Open Mainframe Project thread and it overturned something I had asserted: I had said `EXEC CICS` could not be compiled anywhere available, and Raincode compiles it natively on a PC, free and with no time limit. That correction is exactly why the claim underneath it gets measured rather than believed — the same reasoning as D-97, D-102, D-104, D-106 and D-121, every one of which changed an answer. The single fact in question is cheap to settle and decides three of the four options outright |
| D-130 | **ONFLY's transaction source is written in real `EXEC CICS`, verified against Raincode, not in INTERCOMM macros.** This amends the Phase G row of Section 9.2, which read "ONFLY as a transaction under INTERCOMM on TK5" | Write it in INTERCOMM macros, as the Phase G row said, so that Phase G runs entirely on a real mainframe OS under a real teleprocessing monitor; write it twice, once each way | VL-37 measured the thing that decides it: `EXEC CICS LINK` with ONFLY's own 412-byte COMMAREA runs correctly under the free Raincode, in the MVT-intersection dialect C-03 fixes, with no source change. That is the same source that migrates unchanged to z/OS CICS, which is where the project is going; INTERCOMM-shaped source is throwaway against that destination. The cost is stated rather than discovered later: this source executes on .NET locally, so Phase G's CICS half proves the API and the record layout, not the machine |
| D-131 | **Phase G's 3270 half is driven by INTERCOMM on TK5, not by Raincode.** D-127's requirement that the transaction flow be shown on a real 3270 stands unchanged | Drop the 3270 from Phase G and demonstrate the `EXEC CICS` transaction plus the live view on x86 alone; ask Raincode for a QIX licence and drive the 3270 locally; defer the question until Gate G3 has measured engine speed under Hercules | VL-37 established that the free Raincode cannot drive a terminal at all — `SEND TEXT`, `SEND MAP` and `RECEIVE` are all "not implemented", the terminal server will not start without a `.rclic` the download does not include, and it wants a SQL Server region database besides. VL-32 established that INTERCOMM on TK5 does start, route transactions and close down cleanly. So the only route to a real 3270 that does not depend on a vendor's answer is INTERCOMM, and D-127's reasoning for wanting one is unchanged: an x86 window alone invites the reply that a PC drew the picture. Taken with D-130 this means two thin transaction layers over one engine, which is the cost the owner accepted in exchange for nothing written being throwaway. **This makes TBC-18 blocking**: the SRS already requires it settled before any transaction code is written, and Phase G now depends on INTERCOMM |
| D-132 | **TBC-18 closes: no INTERCOMM-derived material enters the repository.** The MIT tree holds the engine and the `EXEC CICS` transaction source; the INTERCOMM subsystem — its message header, its BTVERB entry, its driver and its JCL — lives only on TK5 and under an ignored working directory, and is never committed. The convention is a top-level `local/` directory, ignored in full, and the rule is enforced mechanically by `tools/lint_lic.py` rather than left to memory | Commit it in a separate non-MIT subtree carrying Tetragon's notice, with the README stating that subtree is not MIT, which would keep the demonstration reproducible from a clone; relicense the whole repository non-commercial to match, reversing D-62; write to Tetragon LLC for a clarification before deciding | VL-38 established the collision exactly: INTERCOMM's clause 1 forbids commercial use of derivative works and MIT grants it, so the two cannot both govern one file, and ONFLY cannot promise onward commercial rights over anything derivative of INTERCOMM. The owner's own use is non-commercial and breaches nothing, so the only thing at stake is what the repository promises third parties — and D-130 has already made that cheap to protect, because ONFLY's real transaction source is `EXEC CICS` and the INTERCOMM side is demonstration scaffolding rather than product. Nothing of value is therefore lost by keeping it out. The cost is stated rather than discovered later: **Phase G's 3270 demonstration will not be reproducible from a clone of this repository**, and whoever wants to reproduce it must obtain INTERCOMM themselves from TK5 and rebuild the shim from the documented layouts. Relicensing was rejected because it reverses D-62 for less clarity than it appears to buy — the vendored SoftFloat and TestFloat stay three-clause BSD regardless, so the tree ends up mixed either way. Writing to Tetragon was rejected as blocking Phase G on an outside party with no published interpretation of the clause to work from |
| D-133 | **NFR-PERF-01's bound is raised from five minutes to ten minutes of wall clock on the TK5 reference host.** The requirement text is amended accordingly; this is the first change to an NFR made on the strength of a measurement rather than a judgement | Keep five minutes, accepting that SR-EXT-03's candidate set is permanently {250, 500} on TK5; keep five minutes for validation and give Phase G's demonstration its own shorter budget | VL-41 measured what the five minutes actually costs: at D-73's 1000 ms trial duration, N=1000 needs 385 s and is excluded by 85 seconds. The bound was chosen before anything had been measured and nothing physical defends it, while N is the dimension the project's credibility rests on — a 1,000-neuron subcircuit is a materially better answer to SR-EXT-01 than a 250-neuron one. **SR-EXT-03's candidate set becomes {250, 500, 1000}**; N=2000 (753 s) and N=4000 (1462 s) remain excluded and no plausible bound recovers them on this host. The costs are stated rather than discovered later: a full acceptance run at N=1000 over six validation rates at 30 seeds is roughly 19 hours, and a ten-minute request is too long to execute in front of an audience, so Phase G's demonstration will need its own duration and N irrespective of this. Which N is finally used is still not this decision's to make — SR-EXT-03 takes the smallest value satisfying ACC-3, NFR-MEM-01 and NFR-PERF-01, and only the third has been measured |
| D-134 | **SUPERSEDED by D-136, which revises the value to 1500 ms.** ~~The maximum simulated duration is 2000 ms, replacing D-37's provisional 5000 ms.** The governing principle is that a request for the maximum must itself be executable inside NFR-PERF-01's bound, which 5000 ms never was | 1500 ms, which is executable at every N in the new candidate set; 1000 ms, equal to the standard duration, forbidding any request longer than Shiu's trial | VL-41 refuted 5000 ms outright: at N=250 it needs 510 s, past even the raised bound, and it was unreachable at every N in the sequence. 2000 ms is twice the trial duration D-73 fixed, which gives room for exploratory runs without inviting requests that cannot finish. **A condition is attached rather than left to be discovered:** 2000 ms is executable within D-133's ten minutes at N=250 (204 s) and N=500 (396 s) but **not at N=1000, where it needs 770 s** — 170 seconds past the bound. So if ACC-3 selects N=1000, either this maximum drops to about 1500 ms or a maximum-length request at that N is knowingly accepted as exceeding NFR-PERF-01, and that is a decision to be taken then, on the evidence, not pre-empted now. Note that NFR-PERF-01 bounds a request at the **standard** duration, so a longer request exceeding it is not strictly a violation; it is nonetheless a request nobody can wait for |
| D-135 | **Seeds per rate stays at 30, and the question is deliberately deferred until the first full acceptance run has been executed.** TBD-06 therefore remains open on this item alone | Reduce to 15 now, halving the suite to about 2.6 hours at N=250; fix 30 permanently and close TBD-06 entirely | The proposal to cut seeds is an argument about variance, and the variance has never been measured. ACC-1 needs a spike in 90% of seeds and ACC-4 compares against one standard error, so cutting the count weakens exactly the criteria the MVP is judged by — and doing that to save wall-clock time, before knowing whether the saving is needed, is the wrong order. The first full run measures the real spread; if it is tighter than feared the count can come down with evidence behind it. The cost is accepted and named: the first acceptance run is the slow one, roughly 5.1 hours at N=250 and about 19 hours at N=1000 over six validation rates |
| D-136 | **SUPERSEDED by D-138 (1300 ms), after VL-42 refuted this value by direct measurement.** ~~The maximum simulated duration is 1500 ms, revising D-134's 2000 ms.** D-133's ten-minute bound stands, reaffirmed after the downstream cost was made explicit | Keep 2000 ms and discard the principle that a maximum-length request must be executable, since NFR-PERF-01 bounds only the standard duration; set the maximum equal to the 1000 ms standard; keep 2000 ms in the header and reject unrunnable requests at run time | D-134 was taken before its interaction with D-133 was worked out, and the two contradicted each other at exactly the N the bound was raised to unlock: 2000 ms costs 770 s at N=1000, 170 s past the bound. 1500 ms is executable at all three candidate N — 153 s at N=250, 297 s at N=500, 578 s at N=1000 — and keeps 50% exploratory headroom above the 1000 ms trial duration D-73 fixed against Shiu. **A reservation is recorded rather than smoothed over.** The margin at N=1000 is 22 seconds, 3.7%, and the 578 s behind it is **extrapolated, not measured**: it is VL-41's 385 s scaled by 1.5. Scaling in steps is far safer than scaling in N, because every step performs identical work, but a 3.7% margin resting on a single-sample measurement with one-second quantisation is thinner than this project's other commitments. VL-42 records the direct measurement; if it lands above 600 s the maximum must come down again, and no N=1000 decision should be taken until it is in |
| D-137 | **The maximum simulated duration is settled by measuring where NFR-PERF-01's bound is actually crossed at N=1000, not by a third estimate.** `tools/mvsprf.py` gains a `--steps` option so successive durations can be measured without editing the source between runs; D-136's 1500 ms stands as refuted until a measured value replaces it | Set the maximum to 1000 ms, the only value already measured to work at N=1000; keep 1500 ms and drop the principle that a maximum-length request must be executable, since NFR-PERF-01 bounds only the standard duration; keep 1500 ms and drop N=1000 from SR-EXT-03 instead | The maximum has now been set twice from estimates and refuted twice by measurement — D-134's 2000 ms by arithmetic against D-133, and D-136's 1500 ms by VL-42 — and VL-42 established why estimating here fails: cost grows super-linearly with duration because activity accumulates, so every interpolation understates it. A third estimate would be the same mistake a third time. The measured bracket is narrow, 1000 ms at 385 s against 1500 ms at 658 s, so one or two runs close it. The cost is named: roughly eleven minutes of lid-open MVS time per point, on a host that has already invalidated two runs tonight by sleeping, and VL-42's certification is what makes a contaminated result detectable rather than silently wrong |
| D-138 | **The maximum simulated duration is 1300 ms, measured rather than estimated, and TBD-06's maximum-duration item closes here.** Taken 2026-09-12 on VL-43's bracket | Measure 1400 ms first, to claim the last ~100 ms of headroom; 1200 ms, for a larger margin against the real network being more active than the synthetic one; 1000 ms, equal to the standard duration, the largest margin of all | 1300 ms is the largest value **directly measured to fit** at N=1000, the tightest of the three candidate network sizes: 507 s against a 600-second bound, a margin of 93 seconds or 15.5%. It keeps 30% exploratory room above the 1000 ms trial duration D-73 fixed against Shiu. Measuring 1400 ms was declined on the evidence rather than on impatience: linear interpolation puts it near 581 s, a 19-second margin of 3%, which is numerically the same thin position D-136 took and VL-42 refuted — and VL-43 measured the cost curve steepening sharply in exactly that region, with marginal cost per millisecond nearly doubling between 1300 and 1500 ms for reasons nothing yet explains. Claiming 100 ms more headroom was not worth a fourth set-then-refute cycle. **What is knowingly accepted:** perhaps 100 ms of theoretical headroom is left unclaimed; and the margin is measured against a *quiet* synthetic network, driven by eight stimulus neurons, so a denser or more active MaleCNS subcircuit could consume some of it — VL-41 records that limit and it applies to this decision in full |
| D-139 | **TBD-19's content: the engine streams spike events for every neuron, plus membrane-potential snapshots for the readout neurons only.** The mechanism and the sampling interval are deliberately left to a separate decision, taken on the volumes this one implies | Spike events alone, the most compact option and the standard raster visualisation; periodic snapshots of all N neurons, showing sub-threshold dynamics everywhere | A spike raster is what neural activity is conventionally drawn as, and it is the same data ACC-1 and ACC-4 already judge, so it comes almost free in interface terms. But spikes alone show only firing, and the sub-threshold build-up toward a spike is much of what makes a brain worth watching. Restricting the state snapshots to the readout set keeps that visible exactly where it matters — the neurons whose behaviour the acceptance criteria turn on — while avoiding the volume of snapshotting everything. **Measured volumes at N=1000 and the 1300 ms maximum (D-138), from VL-43's run:** 538 spikes at 8 bytes each is about 4.2 KB for a whole run; readout snapshots of eight neurons cost about 72 bytes each, so roughly 94 KB at a 10-step interval or 9.4 KB at 100 steps. Against a full per-neuron snapshot at 8 KB apiece — 800 KB for a hundred frames — this is one to two orders of magnitude smaller. **A caveat carried forward rather than discovered later:** those spike counts come from the quiet synthetic network VL-41 describes, driven by eight stimulus neurons, and the spike volume there barely varies with N. A denser or more active MaleCNS subcircuit would emit proportionally more, so the record design must not assume a fixed worst case that this network happens to satisfy. The record layout itself is governed by IR-COM-01, generated from the master definition and never hand-written, and by IR-COM-04, big-endian on every platform |
| D-140 | **TBD-19's mechanism and interval: the caller drives the run in chunks, looping `onfrun` for K steps at a time and emitting between calls, and K travels in the request rather than being fixed in the SRS. TBD-19 closes.** | A callback invoked by the kernel every K steps, cheaper now and needing no change to the run contract; accepting chunking in principle but deferring the kernel change until Phase G is actually planned; a fixed interval in the SRS, avoiding a new request field and any determinism surface | The decisive argument for chunking is not streaming at all. VL-43 measured one request at N=1000 taking **507 seconds**, and Phase H runs ONFLY under CICS, where holding a task for eight minutes is exactly what must not be done — Section 3.7's ICVR runaway-task note says so directly. **The work has to be breakable into pieces for CICS whether or not anything is streamed**, so chunking is a cost already owed to Phase H and the live view comes nearly free on top of it. A callback would leave that problem entirely unsolved and would put an indirect call inside a kernel whose reentrancy NFR-MNT-02 turns on. Chunking also brings a test a callback cannot: **chunked execution must produce a bit-identical result to one monolithic call**, which is an ACC-5-shaped check that would catch any state-carrying defect immediately. A request-borne K lets the demonstration choose smooth motion and the acceptance suite choose cheap, or none, from one binary. **The price, stated plainly:** the generator state must move out of `onfrun`'s local into caller-owned storage and `onfrun` needs an init-or-continue distinction, since it presently re-initialises the whole state structure on every call; FR-SIM-04's guarantee that the sequence is drawn identically everywhere must then hold *across* calls, not merely within one, and its text stays true only if that is honoured. The request gains a field, which is an IR-COM-01 layout change generated from the master definition and never hand-written. **And it creates a determinism surface that must be closed rather than hoped about: the response fingerprint shall not depend on K or on the chunk boundaries**, or ACC-5 fails the moment the demonstration and the suite use different values. That invariant needs its own requirement ID and a test; it is recorded here and proposed, not silently assumed. **Deliberately not implemented now:** Phase G sits behind Phase E and Gates G2 and G4 are open ahead of it, so this is a decision on record, not a change to the kernel tonight |
| D-141 | **Gate G2 opens on the AWS tape image, and integrity is checked by ONFLYENG's existing verify-only mode (IR-TRN-03), which requires taking ONFLYENG to MVS for the first time.** The raw card reader and IND$FILE follow; IR-TRN-02 requires all three | Start with the raw card reader, reusing the submission path already in hand; start with IND$FILE, which needs no Hercules configuration change at all; build the transport artifacts FR-PRP-08 names and defer every transfer to a session with sustained lid-open time. For integrity: a standalone CRC utility built from `engine/src/onfcrc.c`; IEBCOMPR against a known-good dataset | The tape is the lowest-risk change to a lab the parallel session shares — `0:0480 3420 *` has nothing mounted and nothing else uses it, and `devinit` is reversible — while the card reader would mean re-initialising a reader device and IND$FILE depends on a TSO logon and on IND$FILE being present on TK5, which is unverified. Tape is also binary-transparent by construction, so IR-TRN-01 is satisfied by the format rather than by hope, and an 885 KB read takes seconds, so IR-TRN-02's ten transfers cost minutes rather than the hours tonight's compute runs did. **A correction to the options as I put them:** I described verify-only mode as the more expensive integrity check. It is not — **D-78 already built it**, `onflyeng.c` implements `PARM='VERIFY'` and says in its own header that it exists for exactly this, because otherwise checking a transferred file means running a simulation to find out whether the bytes survived. `make eng` exercises it on x86. **The real new work is that ONFLYENG has never run on MVS**: there is no `tools/mvseng.py` and no verification limit records it, so G2's first transfer also becomes the first MVS run of the engine program itself, and a failure will need attributing between the transport and that first port. IEBCOMPR was rejected on a circularity: it needs one trusted copy already on DASD, delivered by a transport whose integrity is the thing G2 exists to establish |
| D-142 | **ONFLYENG selects its integer self-test by backend: the 32-bit suite (TT-01/32) where NR-03's fallback to SoftFloat 2c is in force, the 64-bit suite (TT-01) otherwise, with the ONF901S message naming the width actually run.** `tests/tst32.c`'s self-test is extracted into a unit ONFLYENG can link | Put the width choice inside `softfloat/onfint.c` behind the single name `onfitst()`, leaving ONFLYENG unchanged; skip the self-test on MVS for now and record the NR-14 deviation as an open item, to reach Gate G2's transport answer sooner | VL-45 measured two things at once: GCCMVS cannot compile the 64-bit self-test at any optimisation level, and NR-14 as amended by D-118 never asked it to — the requirement is the self-test "for each integer width that platform's engine actually uses", and on a 2c platform that is 32 bits. So this is not a workaround for a compiler defect; it is implementing the requirement as written, on a program (D-78, D-79) that predates the amendment. Choosing in ONFLYENG rather than inside `onfint.c` keeps the two suites behind two names, so a reader of a passing result can tell from the call site which width was proven — hiding both behind one name would make a green result ambiguous about the thing NR-14 exists to establish. Skipping the test was rejected because it surrenders NR-14's guarantee against risk R-01, a compiler synthesising wrong arithmetic, on precisely the platform whose compiler VL-45 has just shown to be fragile. **Scope stated:** this changes the engine's startup path, so the x86 build must keep selecting the 64-bit suite and `make test` must still pass on all three backends; the existing TT-01/32 vectors are reused unchanged, since VL-30 already records them passing on MVS |
| D-143 | **Gate G2's tape thread continues by testing one hypothesis first: that `LABEL=(1,NL)` is what makes MVS reject the volume, tested by switching to `LABEL=(1,BLP)`.** | Write a standard-labelled image with VOL1/HDR1/HDR2 so MVS has a real label to verify, which is the canonical answer; abandon tape for now and evaluate the raw card reader, IR-TRN-02's second transport; leave G2 and open Gate G4 instead | VL-47 left the tape at a precise and reproducible point: MVS issues the OPEN-time mount `IEC501A M 480,ONFNET,NL,6250 BPI`, the volume is loaded, and MVS answers `IEC502E K` — dismount and keep — then asks again. It is refusing the volume, not failing to see it. The untested hypothesis is that `NL` makes MVS read the first block to confirm it is **not** a label: label processing expects an 80-byte record and `tools/mkaws.py` writes a first block of **32,720** bytes, which would explain a read-then-reject loop. `BLP` bypasses label processing entirely, so it tests that hypothesis directly and changes one JCL operand. It is chosen ahead of the standard-labelled image on cost: a one-operand change against writing and debugging VOL1, HDR1, HDR2 and EOF trailers, when the cheaper experiment would tell us whether labels are the problem at all. **Two outcomes are both useful:** if BLP reads the tape, the hypothesis is confirmed and the question becomes which label form to adopt permanently; if TK5 restricts BLP — it is commonly reserved to authorised job classes, and MVS may silently treat it as NL — the failure will say so and the standard label becomes the next step with one hypothesis eliminated. **Not decided here:** whether BLP is acceptable in the final Phase E JCL. It is a diagnostic, and adopting it would be a separate decision |
| D-144 | **Before writing any more tape-image format code, establish whether the density MVS names in `IEC501A` is the cause of the refusal.** A read-only investigation of what Hercules' 3420 presents, what MVS's UCB believes, and whether the DCB's `DEN` can reconcile them | Write a standard-labelled image with VOL1, HDR1, HDR2 and EOF trailers, the canonical form and the way to test the label hypothesis BLP could not; shrink the first block to a single 80-byte record, testing the same hypothesis far more cheaply; leave tape and evaluate the raw card reader, IR-TRN-02's second transport | VL-48 established that MVS reads the volume and refuses its content, and the refusal message names a density — `IEC501A M 480,ONFNET,NL,6250 BPI,ONFENG,GO`. Two candidate explanations remain and they are not equally cheap to test. The label hypothesis needs either a standard-labelled image, which means writing and debugging four kinds of 80-byte EBCDIC label record, or a change to the block layout that ONFLYENG then has to cope with. The density question needs no new code at all: it is answered by reading what the emulator and the guest each think the device is. Doing the cheap read-only check first is ordering by cost, not by likelihood — **and if density turns out to be the cause, both label options would have been wasted work**. If it is not, the finding eliminates one explanation and the standard label proceeds with better information. Nothing is decided here about which label form ONFLY adopts; this is diagnosis |
| D-145 | **Gate G2's tape is written as a standard-labelled (SL) volume: VOL1, HDR1, HDR2, the data, and the EOF1/EOF2 trailers, read with `LABEL=(1,SL)`.** `tools/mkaws.py` gains the label records | Shrink the first block to a single 80-byte record, keeping NL and testing the same hypothesis with no label formats at all; abandon tape and evaluate the raw card reader; park Gate G2 and open Gate G4 | Four explanations have now been eliminated by measurement rather than argument: the device is online throughout (VL-46), the mount protocol is answered at both of its two requests and behaves identically (VL-48), the density is honoured and makes no difference (VL-49), and the payload cannot be mistaken for a label because it begins ASCII `ONF1` where MVS reads EBCDIC (VL-49). The label hypothesis is what is left. A standard label tests it and, unlike the 80-byte-first-block variant, **is very likely the format ONFLY keeps**: it is what any MVS reads without special JCL, without a job class privileged for BLP — which VL-48 measured TK5 does not grant — and without asking the operator to be trusted about a serial MVS cannot verify. The cheaper experiment was declined because its result would have been a tape shape ONFLYENG must then cope with, and FR-LOD-03 trusts the header's declared length rather than the dataset, so a leading short block is a complication to inherit rather than a format to adopt. **Cost accepted:** four kinds of 80-byte EBCDIC label record, written blind against MVS's expectations, will take iterations. **Not decided here:** whether SL becomes the format of record for IR-TRN-02's ten transfers; that follows from whether it works |
| D-146 | **Gate G2's remaining two transports are both evaluated, the raw card reader next and IND$FILE after it, even though the reader is expected to fail.** | Evaluate IND$FILE next so that a second *viable* transport is compared if time runs short; evaluate only the reader and close IR-TRN-02 on two of three methods, which would need an authorised requirement change; stop Gate G2 with tape measured and open Gate G4 instead | IR-TRN-02 names three methods and IR-TRN-04 selects by comparing them, so a two-method comparison is a different requirement from the one written. The reader is taken first **because it is the one expected to fail**, and an expected failure is worth establishing early rather than carrying as an assumption: both TK5 readers run `ascii trunc`, `000C` is JES2's own submission path, and binary carried as inline `DD DATA` meets JES2's scan for `//` and `/*` — which arbitrary bytes contain by chance. `tools/mvsbld.py` already works around a milder form of this, using `DD DATA,DLM=` because C source begins with `/*`, and no delimiter helps against data that may contain any byte sequence. If that reasoning is right the reader cannot satisfy IR-TRN-01 **in principle**, and demonstrating it is a real IR-TRN-02 result; if it is wrong, a second transport exists and the comparison is richer. Either way the supposition stops being mine and becomes a measurement |
| D-147 | **`onferead()` is rewritten to read without seeking: the 164-byte header first, then exactly the payload length that header declares.** This is FR-LOD-03 as written, and it unblocks the card reader's network half | Read the whole stream to end of file into a growing buffer and validate the length afterwards, which also works on a non-seekable device but still lets the arriving length govern the read; record the defect as a known deviation and finish Gate G2 with tape as the only method that carries the network; fix it and add a regression test reading from a deliberately non-seekable stream | VL-52 measured ONFLYENG rejecting a network the card reader had delivered in full — `devlist` showed `IO[11070]` for 11,068 cards and an empty hopper — with `ONF108E NETWORK DATASET UNREADABLE` raised before any header check. The cause is that `onferead()` begins `fseek(f, 0L, SEEK_END)` and `ftell`, and a card reader is not seekable. **FR-LOD-03 already forbids this**: ONFLYENG shall "trust the header's declared length, not the dataset size". So this is not a new capability but an implementation catching up with a requirement that anticipated the situation; the reader is merely the first device to make the difference observable, because a seekable dataset hides it. The whole-stream variant was declined because it would work while still not doing what the requirement says, which is how the defect survived this long. `ONF_NHDR_LEN` is 164 and `ONF_N_PAYLEN` sits at offset 152, so the header can be read and consulted before the payload is sized. **Scope and risk:** this changes the engine's load path, which the ACC-5 golden suite and TE-01..TE-09 exercise, so all of them must still pass on all three backends; and a declared length read from a not-yet-validated header must be bounded before it reaches `malloc`, since FR-LOD-02 has not run at that point |
| D-148 | **IND$FILE is attempted ONCE, on the test file, and Gate G2's disposition is decided on that result rather than on ten repetitions.** | Restructure for one session carrying all ten transfers, completing IR-TRN-02 as written; record IND$FILE as impractical and close IR-TRN-02 on two methods; park Gate G2 with two transports working and open Gate G4 | Tape and the card reader are each complete — both IR-TRN-02 files, ten transfers each, all passed (VL-51, VL-53) — so Phase E already has more transports than the one it needs. What remains genuinely unknown about IND$FILE is whether the transfer **works at all**, and one attempt answers that; ten answer nothing further about the mechanism and cost ten times the fragility. VL-54 and the `tools/tso3270.py` work established the prerequisites — the module is present, `ws3270` supplies `Transfer()`, and a scripted logon reaches TSO READY — but also measured the session lifecycle being expensive: dropping a 3270 connection does not log TSO off, `LOGOFF` is refused inside ISPF, and after a clean logoff the LU still needs time to settle, with back-to-back attempts failing in two different ways. **That cost is itself evidence for IR-TRN-04's scriptability criterion**, which tape and the reader both satisfy trivially, so it is not wasted whichever way the transfer goes. **What follows is left open deliberately:** if one transfer succeeds, whether IR-TRN-02 may close on two fully-measured methods plus one demonstrated is a requirement question and needs an owner answer, not an inference from this decision |
| D-149 | **IND$FILE is measured in full: ten transfers of the test file and ten of the 885 KB network, so IR-TRN-02 is satisfied exactly as written with no requirement change.** | Close IR-TRN-02 on the evidence as it stood, two methods fully measured and the third demonstrated once, recorded as an authorised requirement change; give IND$FILE ten transfers of the small file only, skipping the network; close Gate G2 and record IND$FILE as demonstrated-but-not-selected, on the reading that ten transfers apply to candidates still in contention | VL-55 proved IND$FILE binary-transparent but on one transfer of one of the two files IR-TRN-02 names, against the full twenty each for tape and the card reader (VL-51, VL-53). The requirement asks for ten per method using the test file **and** a real network file, and meeting it as written costs a session restructure rather than an amendment to the SRS — which is the cheaper of the two things to spend. It also makes IR-TRN-04's comparison rest on equal evidence for all three candidates, so the winner is selected on measurement rather than partly on how much each was measured. **Cost accepted:** 885 KB over a 3270 at the observed 157 Kbytes/sec is slow, and VL-55 measured the session lifecycle to be the fragile part — so the ten transfers will share **one** logged-on session rather than repeating the logon, which is what made the earlier back-to-back attempts fail. Each transfer is still verified individually on the guest by the same `tests/tstxfr.c` the other methods were judged with; transferring ten times and checking once would establish repeatability of neither |
| D-150 | **IR-TRN-04's winning transport is the raw card reader; the AWS tape is recorded as a measured fallback. Gate G2 closes.** | Select the tape, the most conventional route and self-contained through its labelled volume; select IND$FILE, the only method needing no Hercules configuration change and therefore the one that would work unchanged against a real mainframe; select the reader alone and record nothing else | IR-TRN-04 selects in order: integrity, then scriptability from the x86 host, then elapsed time. VL-56 measured **integrity at 20 of 20 for all three**, so the first criterion separates none of them and the second decides. There the reader is decisively simplest — one command, no labels, no volume serial, no mount handshake, no terminal session — against the tape's standard label and **two** mount requests (`IEF233A` at allocation, `IEC501A` at OPEN) and IND$FILE's TSO logon, ISPF exit, `LOGOFF` that ISPF refuses, and process-level retries for LU contention. Elapsed time then confirms rather than decides: 21.2 s against IND$FILE's 21.1 s and the tape's 27.9 s on the 885 KB network. A side benefit worth naming: the network arrives as **11,068 fixed 80-byte cards**, so FR-LOD-03's reassembly from fixed-length records is genuinely exercised rather than reading one block. **The tape is kept as a fallback, and this is not a second winner.** IR-TRN-04 asks for one and gets one; the tape is recorded because it is already fully measured (VL-51), its tooling exists and passes, and it needs no reader device — so Phase E has a route if one is unavailable. FR-PRP-08 requires only the winner's artifacts after this gate, so retaining the tape's is beyond the requirement rather than against it. **Known cost of the winner:** it needs a `devinit` on reader `010C`, so the lab configuration is touched; `$DU` confirmed JES2 drains only `00C`, so nothing contends for it. **IND$FILE's portability argument is recorded and not acted on:** it alone needs no Hercules change and would work unchanged against a real mainframe, which may matter for Phase F — but IR-TRN-04 ranks scriptability above that, and Phase F is not this gate |
| D-151 | **Session scope 2026-09-12 (second session of the day): Gate G4, the COBOL spike S4.** Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion at the start of the session | Start Phase E directly (ONFLYDRV, JCL and BUZZ on the provisional N=913 network) with G4 as its opening step; Phase D, the Linux s390x build, which needs QEMU and an s390x image installed on this host first; the Phase C remainder, blocked at its first step by TBC-06 | G4 is the last open Phase A gate and a hard dependency of Phase E. The TK5 lab was already up, the copybook is already generated (IR-COM-01), and the gate's two questions — does COPY work under MVT COBOL, and does the driver skeleton pass the Enterprise COBOL proxy — fit one session |
| D-152 | **GnuCOBOL is installed on the development host through MSYS2's package manager (`pacman -S mingw-w64-x86_64-gnucobol`, GnuCOBOL 3.2) to serve as the IBM-dialect proxy that Gate G4, FR-BAT-03 and VL-02 name.** This authorises a toolchain install outside the repository; nothing about it enters the tree | Use the already-installed Raincode COBOL compiler as the Enterprise COBOL proxy, which would have been an authorised change to the FR-BAT-03, G4 and VL-02 text; run only the MVT COBOL half on TK5 and report the proxy criterion NOT RUN | Keeps the SRS text exactly as written. MSYS2 and pacman were already present (243 packages installed), so the cost is one package in an existing manager rather than a new one. VL-02 stands: a proxy pass is not proof of Enterprise COBOL compatibility |
| D-153 | **Push policy for this session: commit on the session branch, fast-forward `main`, and push both to `github.com/mertefesensoy/ONFLY` as each step lands.** The owner's goal statement requires everything pushed; the engineering skill's standing "never push" rule is overridden by this explicit answer for this session | Push the session branch only, leaving `main` for the owner to merge; local commits only, following the skill literally and leaving the goal's push constraint unmet | Matches what the history already shows — `origin/main` was at the session branch's head when the session began, so earlier sessions worked the same way. Recorded so that the conflict between the skill and the goal is settled by the owner rather than by either document |
| D-154 | **The Gate G4 plan is approved as presented.** In order: a skeleton `cobol/ONFLYDRV.cbl` in the MVT/Enterprise intersection with `COPY ONFCOM` inside an FD declared `RECORD CONTAINS 412`; the GnuCOBOL IBM-dialect check and a real GnuCOBOL run on x86 whose ONFREQ output is compared byte for byte against the Python layout packing; then one job on TK5 — IEBUPDTE, IKFCBL00 with the copybook library on SYSLIB, IEWL, GO in REQ mode, IDCAMS `PRINT DUMP` of the resulting FB/412 dataset — whose hex dump is compared to the same packing; if COPY fails, the generator gains the inline mode IR-COM-01 already provides for | Change the plan; discuss before starting | The two G4 questions are answered by the two compilers directly, and the byte comparison turns "it compiled" into "it produced the layout the C engine and the oracle expect" (TU-07's driver half), which is what Phase E will actually rely on |
| D-155 | **The G4 skeleton driver does real work in REQ mode — it parses IR-JCL-02 control cards into request records — and only echoes responses in RPT mode.** Names from ONFNAM, Hz formatting, the full message catalog and FR-BAT-05 stay in Phase E | Constructs only, no file logic, leaving the byte-identity check to Phase E; the full FR-BAT-03 driver now | Phase E extends one file rather than replacing a throwaway, and the MVS run produces a real request record whose bytes can be compared, which a constructs-only skeleton cannot give |
| D-156 | **The GnuCOBOL check is a separate `make cob` target that is skipped with a message when `cobc` is absent; it does not join `make test`.** | Add it to `make test` unconditionally | `make test` must stay runnable on a bare checkout, the same rule that keeps the Raincode probes out of it. The target runs wherever GnuCOBOL is found, so the proxy is exercised on this host without becoming a dependency of every checkout |
| D-157 | **ONFLYDRV's single `COPY ONFCOM` lives in WORKING-STORAGE; the ONFREQ and ONFRSP FDs describe their records as `PIC X(412)` with `RECORD CONTAINS 412`, and the driver uses `WRITE ... FROM` and `READ ... INTO`.** This refines D-154, which had placed the COPY inside the ONFREQ FD | COPY in the FD as D-154 said, with RPT mode reading the raw record and addressing no field; two COPYs with every field reference qualified by file name | On OS COBOL an FD's record area exists only while that file is open, so fields copied into the ONFREQ FD are unusable in RPT mode, where ONFREQ is not even allocated; a second COPY duplicates every data name and forces qualification, which C-03 lists as risky under MVT COBOL. The 412-byte check D-154 wanted from the compiler moves to the byte comparison against the Python packing, which also catches a copybook that is too short — something `RECORD CONTAINS` would not |
| D-158 | **The mode card is the first ONFCTL card and reads `MODE=REQ` or `MODE=RPT` in columns 1 to 8; any other first card is ONF401E at card 1.** This is the format FR-BAT-03 left open | Take the mode from the JCL PARM, an authorised change to FR-BAT-03 and an untested MVT COBOL interface; a bare `REQ` or `RPT` in columns 1 to 3 | Fits IR-JCL-02's fixed-column style and cannot be confused with a request card, whose column 1 to 4 field is a stimulus code |
| D-159 | **Numeric control-card fields (rate, duration, seed) may be right-justified with leading blanks or zero-padded; leading blanks read as zeros; an all-blank field is invalid (ONF401E).** Fixes what IR-JCL-02 left open | Zero-padded only; left-justified digits blank-filled | Both natural spellings of a short number work, and a blank field cannot silently become a zero-rate request, which G-01 shows is a meaningful request in its own right |
| D-160 | **An entirely blank control card is invalid and reported as ONF401E; only an asterisk in column 1 makes a comment, exactly as IR-JCL-02 says.** | Skip blank cards like comments | Strict reading of the requirement: every non-comment card is a request. | D-199 | **The fitted-bias plan is approved as presented.** A new diagnostic stage `prep/extract.py --fitbias` scales the measured median-bias rows of D-193 by a multiplier and fits it two ways for N in {500, 1000}: **F1**, out of sample — the multiplier is `a + b*R`, two parameters fitted so the subcircuit's MN9 rate matches the full brain at the three CALIBRATION rates {20, 80, 160}, after which ACC-3 is evaluated on the five VALIDATION rates; and **F2**, in sample — one free multiplier per validation row fitted straight at the ACC-3 targets. The within-run construction is costed and deferred until these numbers are in | Change; discuss | Approved by the owner through AskUserQuestion. **F1 is the one that can carry evidence**: two parameters fitted on three points and tested on five different ones, mirroring D-165's own calibration/validation split. **F2 cannot**, and is labelled so wherever it appears: nearest-rate row selection (D-191) makes each row independent, so F2 is five parameters against ACC-3's five constraints, and a pass measures only whether the mechanism has the freedom, never truncation fidelity. **No new full-brain runs are needed**: `data/calibration/rate-activity.npz` already carries the full brain's MN9 rate at all eight rates over 15 seeds — 5.63 / 46.20 / 76.57 Hz at the calibration rates, which `acc4.json` does not hold — and its validation-rate values 4.57 / 15.07 / 27.63 / 67.73 / 89.63 agree with `acc4.json`'s independent 30-seed run 3.67 / 14.35 / 28.38 / 68.38 / 88.80. ACC-3 is still judged against `acc4.json` at 30 seeds, as VL-66 was. **Engineer's choices that bind nothing:** N in {500, 1000}; 10 seeds for fit candidates and the full 30 for the ACC-3 verdict; the multiplier clipped to [0, 20]. **The within-run cost, stated before the choice:** `runnet` emits per-neuron totals only and would need per-bin counts, the full brain would have to be re-measured with binning, the network format would go to v1.2 with a time dimension, Appendix C would be amended again, and the engine, oracle and tests would follow — comparable to the whole v1.1 effort |
| D-200 | **A third fitted construction, F3: a single CONSTANT multiplier on the measured median-bias rows, fitted on the D-165 calibration rates {20, 80, 160} and judged out of sample by ACC-3 on the validation rates.** One parameter against five test points | Start the within-run compensating input, the remaining D-194 construction; record VL-73 as the finding and settle ACC-3's status, Section 6.4 and the MVS network on seventeen constructions | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. It is the form VL-73's data actually supports: the per-rate multipliers at N = 500 are flat at about **0.58 from 60 Hz up** (0.6032, 0.5519, 0.5781, 0.6455, 0.6235), and F1's line is tilted away from them only because the 20 Hz calibration point wants 0.2570. F1-n500 consequently missed 60 Hz by 13.4% and 200 Hz by 14.2% while landing 40 Hz at 2.4% and 120 Hz at 0.5%. A constant is also **more** parsimonious than F1, not less, so a pass would carry more evidence rather than less. The objective follows SR-CAL-03's own form, the mean relative error over the calibration rates; no rate is excluded, since none of the three has a zero target (D-172's exclusion does not apply). **Engineer's choices that bind nothing:** the same coarse-scan-plus-refine search as VL-73 (D-171's method, required because the response is not monotone), 10 seeds for fit candidates and 30 for the ACC-3 verdict, N in {500, 1000} |

It is the one remaining lead that could make ACC-3 pass **as written**, and the evidence points straight at it: VL-73 measured that at N = 500 no multiplier between 0 and 20 produces more than 0.10 Hz of MN9 firing at a 10 Hz stimulus, while at N = 1000 the same rate is reached and F2-n1000 passes ACC-3 there. The failing rate is therefore a function of how much network is kept, and 500 and 1000 bracket it. **Noted before the choice:** SR-EXT-03's candidate set is {250, 500, 1000}, narrowed from {250, 500, 1000, 2000, 4000} by Gate G3, so 600, 700 and 800 are not admissible values today. Admitting one would be a separate owner decision amending SR-EXT-03; this measurement does not presume it. All three sizes are inside Gate G3's bound, which VL-41 measured to hold up to N = 1000, and far inside TBD-14's 8M |
| D-202 | **ACC-3 is amended: a validation rate whose full-brain reference has a standard deviation at or above its mean is excluded from the criterion and reported instead of tested.** Authorised SRS text change to ACC-3. VL-73 measured that at N = 500 no multiplier between 0 and 20 yields more than 0.10 Hz at a 10 Hz stimulus, and VL-75 that no N between 500 and 1000 recovers it either. **The reason it cannot be met** is in the reference, not the truncation: VL-64 measured the full brain's own 10 Hz response as 3.67 Hz with a standard deviation of 4.22 over 30 seeds — MN9 fires there only in the seeds where the network ignites — while ACC-3's tolerance at that rate is the ±1 Hz floor. The criterion was asking a 500-neuron subcircuit to reproduce the frequency of a rare stochastic event in a 184,099-neuron network to within 1 Hz. **The wording states the reason rather than the rate**, so the exclusion is auditable from data the suite already records and cannot be mistaken for special pleading; it also re-protects the criterion automatically if a later W_syn or stimulus set destabilises a different rate. **Tolerances are not widened** — ±10% and the ±1 Hz floor are untouched, which keeps SR-CAL-05's rule against widening after the fact intact |

ACC-1 is defined on the MVS subcircuit and has never been evaluated on **any** compensated network — the only ACC-1 figure in the register is VL-64's full-brain *preview*, which the SRS itself marks as a preview because the subcircuit did not then exist. Admitting a network on one acceptance criterion while a second that is specifically about it has never been run would be exactly the kind of partial evidence D-189 exists to prevent. ACC-1 tests 40, 60, 120 and 200 Hz — its own text excludes 10 Hz, where the Shiu reference is 0.00 — requiring at least one MN9 spike in at least 90% of seeds and a mean above zero at each. **Engineer's addition, reported separately and binding nothing:** ACC-2 is run on the same network in the same pass, because it is one further engine run and it is the criterion the D-190 compensating input most endangers — a table whose rate 0 row were non-zero would fire neurons with no stimulus |
| D-204 | **Before the N = 500 candidate is admitted, it is run on both soft float backends as well as the native one, and the three are required to agree exactly.** A new `make runners` target builds `runnet` against SOFT3E and SOFT2C beside the existing native build; every validation rate and seed, and the rate 0 request, is run on all three | Amend SR-EXT-01 and SR-EXT-02 and admit the network now (recommended by the engineer); admit it while leaving SR-EXT-01 and SR-EXT-02 unchanged and recording the deviation as an exception | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. **ACC-2's own wording is "on every platform and backend"**, and VL-77 could only report it for x86-64 NATIVE: the format v1.1 compensating table had never been decoded by any build but the native one. Admitting a network on a criterion whose text claims more than the measurement covers is precisely the gap this project's verification-limits register exists to close. The check also exercises the D-190 kernel change — one extra `⊕` per neuron per step — through both soft libraries for the first time, which is what any later MVS fingerprint on this network will have to be compared against, since SOFT2C is the library MVS uses (D-105, NR-03). **It does not discharge ACC-2 or ACC-5 in full:** all three backends here are x86-64, so nothing is proven for s390x, MVS or z/OS |

| D-206 | **Phase C's content is to EVALUATE ACC-3 and ACC-4; passing them is the MVP's bar at Phase E.** Section 9.2's Phase C row records ACC-3 as PASS (VL-76) and ACC-4 as evaluated and failed, recorded as a finding under SR-CAL-05 and risk R-03 (VL-64, VL-65, D-177). **Phase C is complete.** Section 6.4's rule — "the MVP is complete when ACC-1 through ACC-7 all pass" — is untouched and still binds the MVP, which Section 9.2 delivers in Phase E (authorised SRS text change to Section 9.2 only) | Deem ACC-4 satisfied by the act of reporting it, wherever it appears, including Section 6.4's MVP rule; hold Phase C incomplete indefinitely, since nothing can change ACC-4 without reversing D-177; reverse D-177, switching to a dorsal_tpGRN-only stimulus set and recalibrating | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, after asking the engineer to explain ACC-4's significance first. **What ACC-4 is:** the only acceptance criterion that tests ONFLY against *external published science* rather than against itself — ACC-1, ACC-2, ACC-3, ACC-5, ACC-6 and ACC-7 all compare ONFLY with ONFLY. **Why it fails:** both shape clauses pass and magnitude passes at 60, 120 and 200 Hz; it fails at 10 and 40 Hz, where ONFLY fires 3.67 and 14.35 Hz against a reference of 0.00 and 4.73. VL-65 measured the cause — ONFLY drives 14 pharyngeal and taste-peg GRNs on both hemispheres of a **male** connectome where Shiu drove 21 labellar GRNs on one side of a **female** one, and removing either the bilateral drive or PhG9 moves the low-rate end onto the reference. It is a protocol and dataset difference, not a defect. **The SRS anticipated it:** risk R-03 is precisely "MaleCNS cannot be calibrated to match Shiu within ±25% because of dataset differences", whose mitigation reads "Report as a scientific finding; ACC-1 to ACC-3 remain meaningful; owner decides". SR-CAL-05 mandates the report and forbids widening tolerances; D-177 did both. **The distinction the engineer surfaced before the choice:** Section 6.4's all-pass rule is written for the MVP, and Section 9.2 makes the MVP Phase E's deliverable, while Phase C's content merely lists ACC-3 and ACC-4 among the things Phase C does. | D-208 | **Engineer's fix, recorded for the owner rather than decided by him: `prep/calibrate.py` now stores each candidate's exact W_syn and its hexadecimal form beside the four-decimal label, and `prep/extract.py --calcheck` asserts only what is checkable.** No requirement text changes and no result is reinterpreted without the owner | — (not an owner decision; raised here because it corrects a defect in how a *recorded* result was stored, and because it changes how W_syn 0.2969 should be read) | The calibration re-check D-207 called for **failed**, and chasing it down found the reason (VL-79): `key(w) = "%.4f" % w` filed each candidate under a rounded label while emitting its network from the unrounded value, and stored the label as the candidate's `w_syn`. The float the SR-CAL search chose is therefore unrecoverable, and the search is not replayable. Measured here, that matters more than VL-63 supposed: a 0.017% change in W_syn swings the MN9 readout 25% (0.29685 / 0.29690 / 0.29695 give [62, 2] / [80, 4] / [61, 1] at 80 Hz seed 1) while the network total moves 2%. **Nothing downstream is invalidated** — the ranking, ACC-4, the compensating input and the admitted subcircuit all used 0.2969 exactly and consistently — but **W_syn 0.2969 should be read as a value adopted by D-173 from inside a noise-dominated band, not as a measured optimum to four decimals**, and the engineer has flagged that for the owner rather than quietly restating VL-63. Two questions are left open for the owner and not answered here: whether the SR-CAL search should be re-run now that it can be recorded reproducibly, and whether SR-MOD-02's W_syn row should carry this caveat |

If it does not, that is itself the finding. **A new `--log` option** is what makes the re-run real: `evaluate()` returns a cached candidate rather than measuring it, so a re-run against the existing log would have run nothing at all. **No protocol change is made** — changing the seed count to quiet the noise VL-79 found would be a different experiment and a separate owner decision |
