The re-run reproduced the search **exactly** — 16 of 16 candidates, the same bracket, the objective identical to sixteen significant digits — so the question was never whether the search was sound, but which of two values a 0.00515% rounding separates. Measured against the D-164 reference, the adopted literal scores **0.0268** and the search's own choice **0.0691**: the rounding landed 2.6 times better than the minimum golden-section reported. That is not luck to be relied on, it is evidence that SR-CAL-03's objective is noise-dominated at the resolution the search refines to (0.005 mV convergence on a surface where 0.000015 mV changes the answer more), and the owner chose to record that rather than chase a minimum the data cannot resolve. **Nothing downstream changes**; every result already uses 0.2969 |
| D-212 | **The golden suite gains six requests and a network column: G-14, a 60 Hz request on the `path` fixture that closes proposal P-09, and G-15 to G-19 on the admitted `srext` subcircuit.** The five srext requests are chosen to cover what that network adds rather than to mirror the existing thirteen: rate 0 (ACC-2 and the table's mandatory zero row), 40 Hz and 200 Hz (a sampled row selected exactly, and the top row), 9999 Hz (clamping beyond the table) and 30 Hz (D-191's tie rule, equidistant between the 20 and 40 Hz rows) — **the tie request was moved to 100 Hz the same day by D-213**. Authorised SRS text change to Section 8.4 | Mirror all thirteen requests against srext, 26 in total; add a single request, enough for a fingerprint but leaving the compensating table with no golden coverage. **And on P-09:** leave it open; adopt it and put 60 Hz on both networks | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, in one call covering both. **Why five and not thirteen:** the path fixture's thirteen already exercise the engine's edge cases — silence, maximum seed, maximum rate, minimum and maximum duration, seed-zero mapping, reserved and unknown stimulus, out-of-range rate — and none of them touches a compensating table, because `path` has one but no request selects an interesting row. What `srext` uniquely adds is a standing ACC-5 fingerprint for **the network the MVS engine will actually run**, and coverage of every branch of Appendix C step 0. Runtime was not the deciding factor either way: srext is 501 neurons and these runs take seconds. **Why P-09 now:** Section 8.4 was open in front of us and the proposal's reason had never been answered |
| D-213 | **G-19 moves from 30 Hz to 100 Hz.** Both are equidistant between two sampled rates and both exercise D-191's tie rule; 100 Hz sits between the 80 and 120 Hz rows and ties to 80 | Keep 30 Hz and add a second tie at 100 Hz, twenty requests in all; leave G-19 at 30 Hz | Raised by the engineer after checking the new requests were not vacuous, and decided by the owner through AskUserQuestion. At 30 Hz the readouts produce **1 spike and 0** at seed 1, so the fingerprint discriminated the tie rule by a single spike; at 100 Hz they produce **100 and 0**. The check was made because `tests/run_gld.py` records the D-70 trap in its own docstring — the suite once ran on a network whose readouts never fired, four kernel semantics changed under D-67, D-68 and D-69, and not one fingerprint moved. A tie rule tested on one spike is the same trap with a smaller margin |
| D-214 | **Phase D is the work of the 2026-09-14 session: the Linux s390x build, TX-01 and TX-02.** It is taken in the order the dependency column of Section 9.2 already fixes (D-24, D-126); TBD-15 was not reopened | Close D-140's carry-forward obligation instead — give the chunk-invariant fingerprint a requirement ID and a test, which Phase G needs and which requires no new toolchain; close TBD-06's remaining seeds-per-rate question from the 30-seed variance VL-77 now provides; rule on the standing proposals P-08 and P-11 | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. Phase D is the last unmet dependency of Phase E, and it is the first time any ONFLY code will run on a big-endian host: every result in the repository up to this point is x86-64 little-endian, and the whole design — IR-NET-01's big-endian wire format, FR-LOD-05's explicit byte shifts, `onfdec.c`'s decision to leave the payload arrays big-endian and unconverted — is an untested hypothesis about a machine nobody has run |
| D-215 | **The s390x platform for Phase D is a full s390x system VM: Ubuntu 24.04 LTS server, s390x cloud image, booted under `qemu-system-s390x` (machine `s390-ccw-virtio`), with the engine compiled by the guest's own native gcc.** The owner supplies the one privileged step this needs, `sudo apt install cloud-image-utils` in WSL Ubuntu, because the engineer must not handle a password | Install `gcc-s390x-linux-gnu` on the x86 host and run the resulting binaries under the `qemu-s390x` user-mode emulator already present, which needs no guest at all and would have started compiling within minutes; boot Alpine Linux s390x netboot, which needs no sudo and no cloud-init seed but puts root in RAM and uses musl rather than glibc; have the engineer hand-write the cloud-init NoCloud seed filesystem in Python to avoid the sudo step | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, in two rounds: the platform first, then the guest once `sudo -n true` was measured to return `sudo: interactive authentication is required` and the cross-compiler was ruled out. A full system VM runs a real s390x kernel, a real glibc and a real ELF loader, so a result under it is a statement about Linux on z/Architecture rather than about QEMU's user-mode syscall translation layer. **It does not make the result hardware-true:** VL-01 stands, QEMU emulates s390x floating point in software, and nothing here is evidence about a real z machine |
| D-216 | **TX-01 and TX-02 run against a synthetic network and all four emitted networks — `srext`, `path`, `hop2` and `full`.** | Synthetic plus `srext` and `path` only, the two the golden suite of Section 8.4 actually names, leaving the two large networks with no s390x evidence; synthetic plus `srext` alone | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The two large networks are where a big-endian defect would hide: `full` is 184,099 neurons and 299,515,264 bytes, large enough to cross every size and offset boundary the 913-neuron fixture never reaches, and it is the only network with `nbias = 0` and therefore no compensating-input table at all. **Known cost, accepted:** Section 8.4 defines no golden request against `hop2` or `full`, so their cross-platform comparison is an x86-versus-s390x fingerprint identity check rather than a standing golden entry; and at the 8.5 M neuron-steps per second measured on x86 (VL-41's host), one 1000 ms full-brain run is 1.84 G neuron-steps, so under TCG emulation the duration for the two large networks may have to be cut. Any such cut is a further decision, not an assumption |
| D-217 | **The worktree is exported to the guest read-write over QEMU's 9p virtfs.** The guest compiles from the repository's own sources and reads `data/networks` in place; its outputs land in the repository | Export the same share read-only and carry results out as base64 on the serial console, which keeps the VM unable to touch the repository; ship a tarball in on a raw virtio disk and carry results out on a second disk, with no live share at all | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The deciding quantity is the 299,515,264-byte `full` network that D-216 requires: a live share reads it in place, while both alternatives copy it at least once per iteration. **Accepted risk:** a guest with write access to the worktree can corrupt it, so FR-PRP-09's reproducibility and the `data/networks/MANIFEST.json` digests are re-verified on the host after every guest session |
| D-218 | **The Linux s390x build is the existing `Makefile`, parameterised — not a second build file.** The x86-only settings move into a platform block selected by a variable; every rule stays shared | Extract the shared rules into a `build/linux.mk` overlay; write a standalone `Makefile.s390x` | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. Measured before asking: of the 585 lines in the Makefile, **exactly one is x86-specific** — `NATFLAGS` at line 46, which carries `-msse2 -mfpmath=sse` for D-30's 32-bit mingw x87 problem and `-DONF_FP_LITTLE` for the byte swap in `onffpn.c`. The 70 `.exe` suffixes are cosmetic on Linux and no Python tool in `tools/` or `tests/` contains a Windows-ism. Duplicating 585 lines to vary one of them is the drift IR-COM-01 forbids for layouts, applied to the build instead |
| D-219 | **TX-01 in Phase D compares the s390x response records against the x86 build's, byte for byte, and is reported PARTIAL.** The MVS half of "Linux s390x and MVS response records are identical" is deferred to Phase E, when an MVS engine build exists for the first time | Report TX-01 as NOT RUN until Phase E, evaluating only TX-02 now; amend Section 8.5's TX-01 text to read "identical across every platform in the determinism matrix" | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. No SRS text changes: TX-01 keeps the wording it has, and the report says plainly which half was proven. The x86 records are the right comparand in the interim because they are what the MVS build will itself have to match — if s390x and x86 disagree, Phase E cannot succeed either, and finding that now costs hours instead of days inside Hercules |
| D-220 | **All three float backends run on s390x: SOFT3E, NATIVE and SOFT2C.** Rows 4 and 5 of the Section 8.3 determinism matrix name only the first two; SOFT2C is run in addition, and its s390x results are reported as evidence beyond what ACC-5 requires rather than as a new matrix row | Run only SOFT3E and NATIVE, exactly what rows 4 and 5 require | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. SOFT2C is the library the MVS engine actually uses (NR-03, D-105, VL-21) and it has **never been executed on a big-endian machine** — every 2c result in the repository is x86-64. Release 2c reassembles binary64 from 32-bit halves, so it is precisely the kind of code where a host-endianness assumption hides. Finding such a bug here costs an edit; finding it in Phase E costs a Hercules debugging session. **This does not change ACC-5's row set**, which stays the owner's to change (see P-08) |
| D-221 | **The FR-BAT request/response record loop is built in Phase D, ahead of the Phase E placement Section 9.2 gives it, so that TX-01 can be evaluated against something real.** This is a deliberate ordering deviation, authorised by the owner; Section 9.2's text is not changed | Report TX-01 as NOT RUN in Phase D and prove the big-endian record layout only through TU-07's `tstcom` and ONFLYENG's manifest (recommended by the engineer, as the option that pulls no Phase E work forward); report TX-01 as NOT RUN and confine Phase D to TX-02 and TX-03 | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, after the engineer discovered and reported that **the premise of D-219 was wrong**: D-219 had the s390x records compared against the x86 build's, but `engine/src/onflyeng.c` states in its own header that "The request/response loop is NOT here", D-78 having built only IR-TRN-03's verify-only mode. No build on any platform emits a response record, so there was no x86 comparand to compare against. The owner's choice makes TX-01 evaluable rather than deferred, and it is the earliest point at which the record layout that Phase E and Phase G both depend on gets exercised on a big-endian machine. **D-219 still stands** as to the comparand and the reporting: s390x is compared against x86, and TX-01 is reported PARTIAL because the MVS half cannot exist until Phase E |
| D-222 | **The plan for D-221's loop is approved as presented:** a new `engine/src/onfreq.c` module holding one request-processing entry point; a run mode added to ONFLYENG that loops ONFREQ into ONFRSP with ONF301I per request, ONF302I as the summary and the step return code of IR-JCL-04; `tools/mkreq.py` to turn IR-JCL-02 control cards into an ONFREQ file; `tests/run_req.py` to drive the Section 8.4 suite through the loop against the oracle; and Makefile wiring on all three backends | Build a smaller version that emits ONFRSP records for the golden requests only, skipping the control-card parsing and the ONF301I/ONF302I messages — faster to s390x but leaving IR-JCL-02 and FR-BAT untested; discuss the design before any code | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The smaller version was offered because Phase D's real target is the big-endian comparison, not the batch pipeline; the owner took the full build, which means FR-BAT-01's STEP2, FR-BAT-05, IR-JCL-02, IR-JCL-03 and IR-JCL-04 all acquire their first implementation and their first test here rather than in Phase E |
| D-223 | **The Linux and x86 builds name the request and response datasets as `argv[3]` and `argv[4]`.** This extends D-86's existing convention, in which `argv[1]` is the PARM and `argv[2]` the network dataset. On MVS these remain the DD names ONFREQ and ONFRSP; FR-LNX-01's "ordinary files" are these two positional arguments | Read the two paths from environment variables named ONFREQ and ONFRSP, which reads more like DD names; look for fixed filenames ONFREQ and ONFRSP in the working directory, the closest spelling to MVS | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The deciding property is evidence: every command this project reports has to be reproducible from the transcript, and a positional argument is visible in the command line while an environment variable is not. Fixed filenames were the closest to MVS but make two concurrent runs collide silently |
| D-224 | **The request-validation and fingerprint sequence is extracted into the `onfreq` module and shared: `tests/tstgld.c` is changed to call it rather than keeping its own copy.** | Leave `tstgld.c` untouched and write the sequence a second time in `onfreq.c` | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The sequence is an *ordering* — unknown stimulus before reserved stimulus before rate range before NR-12's `rate × dt ≤ 1,000,000` bound before duration range, and the fingerprint computed over whatever that ordering produced — and two copies of an ordering do not diverge loudly. A drift would surface as a changed fingerprint on one path only, which is exactly the class of defect the golden suite exists to catch and would instead be caused by. The accepted cost is that `tests/tstgld.c`, which passes today, is modified; the golden suite re-run on all three x86 backends is what protects that |
| D-225 | **`testfloat_gen` is built for s390x out of tree, from ONFLY's own Makefile, so that TT-02 and the `c2c` check run natively on the platform under test.** The TestFloat sources are compiled from `third_party/` with an ONFLY-owned `platform.h` supplied on the include path; **no file is added under `third_party/`**, so D-28 and D-35's byte-identity with the published archives holds and `third_party/MANIFEST.md` stays true | Run the Win32 generator on x86, write its cases to files and teach `tests/run_tt02.py` to read a file instead of spawning a generator; extend the baked-in `generated/onftfv.h` header pattern that MVS uses (VL-29) from SOFT2C to SOFT3E and NATIVE; vendor a new `build/Linux-s390x-GCC` directory upstream-style | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. **Correction to the cost stated when the question was asked:** the engineer reported that `testfloat_gen` links only TestFloat objects and needs SoftFloat for headers alone. That was a misreading of an excerpt — the list read was `OBJS_COMMON`, the contents of `testfloat.a`, not the link line. Line 307 of the vendored Makefile is `testfloat_gen$(EXE): $(OBJS_TESTFLOAT_GEN) testfloat$(LIB) $(SOFTFLOAT_LIB)`, so a SoftFloat **library** for s390x is required as well. The decision is unaffected and the work is still small, because **both** vendored Makefiles parameterise `SOURCE_DIR`, `SPECIALIZE_TYPE` and `PLATFORM` with `?=` and put `-I.` — the build directory — first on the include path. That is upstream's own mechanism for a new platform: a build directory containing nothing but a `platform.h`. ONFLY supplies that directory outside `third_party/` and drives the vendored Makefiles from it, so no source list is copied and none can drift. **Why a native generator rather than carried vectors:** NR-14 asks the TestFloat suite to pass *on the platform*, and a vector file produced on x86 is an artifact the s390x run trusts rather than produces. Generating on s390x means the operand stream, the reference results and the comparison all come from the machine whose arithmetic is in question |
| D-226 | **Appendix E gains ONF906S, RC 16: REQUEST DATASET UNREADABLE OR NOT A MULTIPLE OF 412.** Emitted by ONFLYENG when the ONFREQ dataset cannot be opened or read, or when its length is not a whole number of 412-byte records (authorised SRS text change: one new Appendix E row) | Add ONF204E at RC 8, in the 2xx request range beside ONF202E and ONF203E; reuse the existing ONF108E at RC 12 and name the path, adding no catalog entry at all | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The 9xx range is environment and severe failures, which is what a missing or truncated dataset is — it mirrors ONF108E's treatment of the network dataset rather than pretending a whole unreadable file is a bad *request*. RC 16 also matters operationally: IR-JCL-04 conditions STEP3 on STEP2 ending below 12, so 16 stops the report step, whereas the RC 8 alternative would have let STEP3 print a report over a dataset that was never read. Reusing ONF108E was rejected because its text says NETWORK DATASET, and an operator message that names the wrong dataset is the failure mode NFR-REL-01 exists to prevent |
| D-227 | **ONF905S is kept in Appendix E and annotated as historical.** No build that contains D-221's loop can emit it; the entry stays because it describes the D-78 engine level, which is what any already-transported MVS binary still is (authorised SRS text change: annotation of one existing Appendix E row, no change to its text, code or RC) | Remove the row now that nothing can emit it; keep the code but re-purpose it to mean "no request dataset was supplied" | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. Deleting it would leave a message that D-78-level binaries can still print undocumented, and Phase E has not yet built an MVS engine at all, so those binaries are not hypothetical. Re-purposing was rejected for the same reason D-226 rejected reusing ONF108E: a code that has meant one thing in a recorded result must not quietly start meaning another |
| D-228 | **TE-09's "without simulating" clause is proved two ways at once.** *Observationally:* ONFLYENG is run under PARM='VERIFY' with a valid ONFREQ dataset deliberately supplied, and must print ONF003I, print no ONF301I or ONF302I, never create the ONFRSP file, and return 0. *Structurally:* a second binary is built with the request loop compiled out, and `nm` must still show `onfrun` absent from it | Observational checks only, dropping the structural argument the kernel link made impossible; structural only, keeping the original argument but testing a binary that is not the one shipped | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. D-221 linked the kernel into ONFLYENG, which destroyed the argument `tests/run_eng.py` relied on — "a program that does not contain onfrun cannot have called it". The observational half is what now covers the **shipped** engine, and it is not a timing measurement, which the original author had rejected: if verify-only had simulated, it would have written response records, so the absence of the ONFRSP file is direct evidence. **Consequence, recorded rather than hidden:** the structural half needs a compile-time switch to omit the loop, and a binary built that way reaches exactly the path that emits **ONF905S**. So that message is no longer unreachable as D-227 anticipated — it is emitted and tested by the TE-09 variant. Appendix E's annotation is corrected to say so |
| D-229 | **D-216's cross-platform runs on `hop2` and `full` use three requests each: SUGR at 0, 40 and 200 Hz, seed 1.** These are not additions to Section 8.4's golden suite — that suite stays at G-01…G-19 — but a Phase D comparison set, recorded under `data/phase-d/` | One request each, SUGR 40 Hz seed 1, the minimum that exercises decode, load, kernel and fingerprint; the same five requests `srext` carries (0, 40, 200, 9999 and 100 Hz) | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. Section 8.4 defines no request against either network, so one had to be chosen. Rate 0 is ACC-2's silence condition, and 40 and 200 Hz are D-165 validation rates at low and high drive, so the kernel is compared across platforms rather than only the decoder. The five-request option was rejected on the engineer's observation that two of them — 9999 Hz and the D-191 tie rate — exercise compensating-table row selection, and **`full` carries no table at all** (`nbias = 0`), so on that network they would test nothing the other three do not |
| D-230 | **`hop2` runs at the 1000 ms standard duration; `full` runs at a short duration, the exact value to be proposed from the measured per-step rate and decided separately.** | Standard 1000 ms for both, accepting hours per request under TCG emulation; a short duration for both | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The reasoning the owner accepted: a big-endian defect on a 299,515,264-byte network with 24,775,486 edges would be a misread offset or CSR row pointer — that is in the **decode and load**, whose cost is the same whatever the duration, while the steps add only wall-clock. `hop2` at 13,521 neurons is cheap enough that no such trade is needed. **This decision deliberately does not fix the short duration**: it was taken on estimates (8.5 M neuron-steps per second on VL-41's x86 host, so 1.84 G steps for one full-brain second) and the engineer had not yet measured the TCG ratio |
| D-231 | **D-147's payload sanity bound moves from `engine/src/onflyeng.c` into `engine/include/onfplat.h` and becomes platform-dependent:** 64 MB where the platform is MVS or CMS, larger elsewhere. D-147's value and its reasoning are unchanged on the platform it was written for | Raise the bound globally to one larger constant; use `tools/runnet.c` for the full network instead, giving it a fingerprint comparison but no response-record comparison; drop `full` from D-216 | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. D-216 asks TX-01 and TX-02 to run against all four networks, but ONFLYENG refused to read `full` at all: its payload is about 299 MB against D-147's 64 MB bound, so the run ended in ONF108E. The two decisions were in direct conflict and the engineer did not resolve it alone. **Why the platform header is the right place:** NFR-PRT-01 permits exactly one file to differ between platforms and `onfplat.h` is it, so this does not fork the engine source. **Why the bound is not simply raised:** D-147 exists because the declared length is read from a header FR-LOD-02 has not yet validated, and C-01 gives the MVS region single-digit megabytes — the guard is real where the region is small and theatre where it is not. `hop2`, at 20,615,112 bytes, was never affected |
| D-232 | **D-230's open value is fixed: the `full` network runs at 100 ms in the Phase D comparison set.** `hop2` stays at the 1000 ms standard duration | 50 ms, half the cost; 200 ms, the strongest evidence at twice the cost; 1000 ms, the standard duration, at many hours per backend under emulation | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, on a measurement that changed what the question was. The engineer first recorded `full` at 1 ms and found the run **vacuous**: both readout entries came back `(id, -1, 0)`, never firing, so the three fingerprints differed only because the rate is a field of the request and not because the simulation had done anything. That is the D-70 trap — a network whose readouts never fire cannot detect a kernel change — reappearing in a new place. Measured at 200 Hz, seed 1, x86-64 NATIVE, first spikes at **26.0 ms and 39.0 ms**: 50 ms gives 4 and 1 readout spikes (18.5 s), 100 ms gives 11 and 2 (35.9 s), 200 ms gives 28 and 5 (74.3 s). 50 ms was rejected for the reason D-213 moved G-19 off 30 Hz: a comparison that turns on a single spike has almost no margin |

This is the pattern D-156 already set for GnuCOBOL: `tests/run_cob.py` prints a skip line and exits 0 when no `cobc` is found, so a bare checkout stays runnable. Two prerequisites are genuinely absent in the s390x guest: `git ls-files` fails because `/onfly/.git` is a *file* pointing at a Windows path the guest cannot resolve, and `tests/test_signs.py` needs pandas while `tests/test_gain.py` needs numpy. **Neither check is platform-dependent** — `liclint` reads the git index for the D-132 licence rule, and the `prep` tests exercise a pure-Python preparation pipeline whose results cannot differ by host — so running them on s390x would have proved nothing that x86 does not already prove. A reduced target list was rejected because the exclusion would then exist only in the engineer's head and in prose; a printed skip line puts it in the build output where it cannot be forgotten |

The sign is honoured for the rate only, at the two call sites rather than inside the shared conversion paragraph; a minus in the duration column is still rejected exactly as before. This is stated because the paragraph is shared and a reader would otherwise reasonably expect both fields to have changed |

The bare type was declined because **both** `srext` readouts are MN9, so FR-BAT-04's report would print two rows a reader cannot tell apart; the ordinal was declined because it is an artefact of this network's neuron ordering and means nothing outside it, where the body id is the dataset's own key. The form is legal under IR-NAM-02 — A–Z, 0–9, underscore, hyphen and period — and is 9 characters in a 34-column field |

| D-270 | **FR-BAT-04's "return code with its message" is resolved by mapping IR-JCL-04's return code to Appendix E, disambiguating RC 8 from the stimulus code: a code that is not one of SUGR, WATR or BITR gives ONF203E, any other gives ONF202E** | One generic text per return code, dropping the ONF202E / ONF203E distinction the catalog defines; add a message-identifier field to the response record so the engine states it outright | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The response record carries only the return code — 0, 4, 8 or 16 (`ONFR_OK`, `ONFR_WARN`, `ONFR_ERR`, `ONFR_SEV` in `engine/include/onfreq.h`) — and Appendix E maps **8 to two messages**. The driver's rule cannot disagree with the engine because `engine/src/onfreq.c` tests `stimid == ONF_STIM_UNKNOWN` **first**, before any range check, so a request that reaches RC 8 with a recognised code can only have failed a range check. The record-field option was declined on cost: it changes the 412-byte layout, hence `ONF_RECLEN`, every generated artifact, and every recorded response file including the MVS results taken earlier the same day |

Hard-coding would put the codes in two places, which is the drift IR-COM-01 created the generated copybook to prevent |

Truncation would print 13.2 for a value of 13.33; the owner chose the figure a reader expects of a rounded rate |

An ACC-6 result that is quietly too large would size nothing wrongly on its own, but one taken across a sleep is not a measurement at all. Because this touches a tool an exit criterion is read from, it was asked rather than done. **TX-04 is re-run afterwards** so the reported ACC-6 comes from the guarded tool |
| D-313 | **ACC-4 is investigated further before the session closes, and the investigation is the shape test on the SHIPPED D-52 stimulus set** — not on a variant — measuring W_syn sensitivity at 10, 40, 60 and 120 Hz | Probe the `right` variant, closing VL-65's other half; both, shipped set first; a 4-seed screen of the shipped set before committing to 30 seeds | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. VL-103 established that no W_syn passes ACC-4 **for the tpgrn variant**, which is a road D-177 declined to take; whether the same is true of the configuration ACC-4 is **actually evaluated on** was never measured. That is the question bearing on Phase E and on risk R-03. **Seed count 30, following D-309's precedent within the option chosen** — a four-seed pass already produced a wrong mechanism once (VL-100 corrected by VL-103), so the cheaper screen was not taken. Rates 10 and 40 are ACC-4's two failing rates and 60 carries the tightest floor, so the four bracket the feasible window from both sides. 480 full-brain runs, about four hours |
| D-314 | **The session closes on the final test run, `git diff --stat` and the goal report, with no additional ceremony** — the labs are left running and no separate Phase E completion document is written | Shut both labs down first, as D-293 did, before reporting; write a Phase E completion document covering the whole phase beyond the per-slice ones | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The per-slice implementation documents and Appendix D already carry every result with its command, its platform, compiler and backend, and its limits; a further summary would restate them. **The labs are therefore still up at the end of this session** — TK5 under Hercules and the QEMU s390x guest — which the next session must know, because D-293's precedent is that they are shut down cleanly before the owner travels with the machine |
| D-315 | **Long-running host jobs report progress the owner can watch without asking: `prep/acc4.py` and `prep/wsens.py` are made to flush, long runners write `data/progress/<job>.json` atomically, and `tools/progress.py` prints or `--watch`es them.** Standard library only; no dependency | Fix only the flush, leaving the owner to tail raw text; add a local web dashboard on `http.server` with a polling HTML page as well; defer all of it as a follow-up and keep the session on Phase E | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. **The flush is a real defect, not a missing feature:** `prep/extract.py` calls `sys.stdout.flush()` in five places and its logs appear immediately, while `acc4.py` and `wsens.py` never flush, so Python block-buffers about 8 KB and a redirected log reads **0 bytes until the process exits** — which is why a 6,971 s ACC-4 run and an 11,047 s probe were invisible for their whole duration. The progress file is what makes the state readable by something other than a human reading a log |
| D-316 | **TBD-19's carried obligation is closed now rather than in Phase G: the fingerprint's independence from the chunk size K gets a requirement ID, proposed in Appendix A.2 for the owner to accept, and a test** | Write the test now against current behaviour and leave the requirement wording to Phase G; leave the whole obligation to Phase G, where it properly belongs | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. D-140 breaks a run into chunks so that Phase H's CICS path need not hold a 507-second task, and TBD-19 closed recording that the fingerprint must not depend on K or on where the chunk boundaries fall. **ACC-5 is the project's spine** — nineteen fingerprints reproduced across seven platform, compiler and backend rows — so a chunk-dependent fingerprint would not cost a feature, it would cost the determinism claim, and it would be discovered in Phase G with Phase E's evidence already published. No Phase G code is written: the requirement is proposed, not enacted, and the test states the property Phase G must satisfy |
| D-317 | **The chunk-invariance obligation is tested in two stages: a narrow test first, taking no risk, then a minimal refactor of the oracle to make its state resumable so the whole property can be proved.** The golden suite is re-run on all three x86 backends afterwards to show no fingerprint moved | The narrow test alone, which pins the likeliest failure mode and cannot perturb anything; the refactor alone; only the requirement wording, leaving every test to Phase G | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The constraint driving the question is that **nothing in the tree can currently run in chunks at all**: `oracle/onfly_oracle/kernel.py`'s `run()` builds `u`, `g`, `rfr`, the delay ring and the generator internally and loops over `steps`, with no `step()` and no way to resume. So the full property cannot be asserted without something carrying state across a boundary. The narrow stage covers the likeliest failure — a chunked driver that re-seeds per chunk would break FR-SIM-04's single PRNG stream — with **no change to O-1, the bit-exact reference**. The second stage edits O-1, which is why it is staged behind the first and why ACC-5's nineteen fingerprints across three backends are the check that it changed nothing |
| D-318 | **P-15 is adopted as written: FR-SIM-10 enters Section 3.3, and Section 8.5's catalogue gains TU-10 against it.** This is an authorised change to SRS **requirement** text | Adopt it but scope it to the fingerprint alone, dropping "the response shall be identical"; adopt it but file it under IR-COM rather than FR-SIM; leave it a proposal and add no requirement text until Phase G is planned | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, with the three arguable drafting choices put in front of them: requiring the whole **response** to match rather than only the fingerprint (the fingerprint excludes text by IR-COM-05's design, so a fingerprint-only rule would permit two responses differing in an excluded field); covering **every placement of chunk boundaries** and not only a fixed K, since D-140 lets K travel in the request and a caller could vary it mid-run; and placing it in FR-SIM because it constrains the simulation contract, though IR-COM-05's fingerprint is what it protects. **The cost was stated before the choice:** this adds a requirement the engine does **not** satisfy today, `engine/src/onfker.c` having no chunked entry point at all, so FR-SIM-10 is met by the oracle (VL-107) and open against the engine until Phase G builds that path |
| D-319 | **`tools/progress.py` infers progress for jobs that have no tracker file, and gains a one-line `--brief` form.** Inferred output is labelled as such and never presented as measured | Leave it, since the tracker already covers every future job and the one now running would stay opaque for its remaining two hours; add the inference without the one-liner | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, whose stated need was to stop having to ask. The gap is real and recurs: **a job already in flight when the tooling changed has no tracker file**, which is exactly the 480-run shape test running now, and a tracker whose write fails goes silent by design (D-315). What remains observable in that case is the filesystem and the process table — which `net-<w>.bin` a run has emitted and is therefore working on, its mtime, and how many `runnet` workers are alive. **The worker count is taken by process NAME, never `pgrep -f`:** `-f` matches whole command lines and the shell running the search contains the pattern, so it finds itself, which produced a contradictory reading during the 2026-09-15 shutdown |
| D-320 | **`prep/acc4.py` deletes the candidate network it emits, with a `--keep` spelled exactly as `prep/calibrate.py` spells it.** **Instructed directly by the owner** ("fix acc4.py to clean up after itself") rather than asked, the owner having raised it themselves | Leave the behaviour and document that ACC-4 abandons its network; delete without offering `--keep` | `prep/calibrate.py` has always deleted its candidates — `if not keep: os.remove(path)` at the end of `evaluate()` — and `acc4.py` emits the same **299 MB** network through the same `cal.emit_candidate()` and never did, so every ACC-4 run since Phase C has abandoned 299 MB. **How it was found is worth recording:** `tools/progress.py` gained inference under D-319 and reported a network ACC-4 had abandoned 15.8 hours earlier as a job 15.8 hours into its current W_syn point. The leftover was not the bug that tool was written to find; it was one it tripped over. The deletion happens **after** the runs, the file being what every worker reads, and the network is regenerable byte-for-byte from the signed cache and W_syn with its SHA-256 and CRC-32 in the manifest, so nothing is lost. A failure to delete is **reported, not raised**: a three-hour run whose science is complete must not fail over housekeeping. `tests/test_disc.py` pins all three behaviours |
| D-321 | **The abandoned `data/calibration/net-0.2969.bin` is deleted.** An irreversible action, so it was asked rather than taken as an obvious consequence of D-320 | Leave it, `tools/progress.py` now labelling it LEFTOVER so it cannot mislead again; sweep `data/calibration` for every abandoned `net-*.bin` and remove them all | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The file is **299 MB** written 2026-09-15 at 16:11 by the ACC-4 re-run of VL-97, before D-320 made `acc4.py` clean up after itself. **It is regenerable byte-for-byte** from the signed cache and W_syn 0.2969 through `cal.emit_candidate`, and its SHA-256 and CRC-32 are recorded in the run's own record, so deleting it destroys no evidence. **Checked safe against the job running at the time:** the D-313 shape test labels its candidates `d52`, so its own 0.2969 point writes `net-d52-0.2969.bin` and never reads this file |
| D-322 | **The progress CLI shows what a running job is doing INSIDE, read from the worker processes themselves — which rate, which seeds, how many runs dispatched and finished — rather than a one-line summary.** The owner's words: *"i want to see what is going on inside. The english one-liner didn't really satisfy me by itself"* | Keep the one-liner as it was; cover the TK5/MVS jobs instead; send a notification on completion instead of showing progress | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, asked a third time at their request. **The data was there from the start and was not looked for:** `prep/acc4.py`'s `run_many` spawns one process per run, `runnet <network> <rate_hz> <sim_ms> <seed>`, so the process table states exactly which (rate, seed) pairs are in flight and against which network. Because the dispatch order is `[(r, s) for r in rates for s in seeds]`, the furthest-advanced worker gives the **exact** dispatch position within the current W_syn point — no timing estimate is involved. **Dispatched and finished are reported separately** because they are different facts: a worker appears when it starts and vanishes when it exits, so finished is dispatched minus those still alive, which is exact, rather than a percentage that blurs the two. **One defect this exposed immediately:** keying the display off the network file alone reported *"no jobs running"* during the ~76 s gap between W_syn points, when the finished point's network has been deleted and the next has not been written — so a four-hour run looked finished twice an hour. A job is a process, not a file; the process list is now the spine and the file only says which point |
| D-323 | **A long job opens its own progress window and the watcher outlives it: `prep/wsens.py` and `prep/acc4.py` spawn a terminal in the worktree running `progress.py --watch 15 --until-done`, which ends with a `JOB FINISHED` banner in a window that stays open. The view shows the computation itself — a live run log, and for jobs started after this, what each run returned.** `--no-window` suppresses it | Leave the owner to open a window and start the watcher by hand each time; show only the summary and not the per-run detail | Instructed directly by the owner across two messages: *"when you run the job it should automatically open a terminal window ... it should track it until the job is finished ... print a JOB FINISHED"* and *"i still want to see the computations and logs happening inside the job so not just a small summary"*. **Two sources of detail, and they are not interchangeable.** From OUTSIDE a job, diffing the live worker set each poll gives a real event stream — every run starting and finishing, with its duration — and that works on a job already in flight. What it cannot give is what a run **returned**: a worker's output goes to the parent's memory and is written only when the whole job ends, so for the 480-run job running when this was built the MN9 numbers are unreachable. `Tracker.log()` closes that for jobs started afterwards, `acc4.run_many` recording each run's spike counts as it lands. The window is default-on because watching should not depend on anyone remembering, and `-NoExit` keeps the banner on screen hours later |
| D-324 | **The session continues rather than closing on ACC-4's failure: there is more to measure.** The candidates are the `right` variant probe (VL-65's other half), D-274's SUGR job, and the external-clock guard on ACC-7 | Amend ACC-4's text and report the phase done; accept the finding and close the goal as blocked on a decision engineering cannot resolve; re-scope to a phase whose exit criteria are all achievable | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, after the engineer put the bind plainly: the goal's DONE requires every Phase E exit criterion listed as PASS, ACC-4 is a measured FAIL, and **no principled amendment rescues it**. There is precedent for excluding a rate whose reference is unusable — D-172 excluded zero-reference rates from SR-CAL-03's objective and D-202 excludes rates where the reference's standard deviation exceeds its mean from ACC-3 — and applying it would excuse **10 Hz**, whose Shiu reference is exactly 0.00. It would **not** excuse **40 Hz**, whose reference of 4.73 is perfectly usable against ONFLY's 14.35. Inventing a new exclusion to fit a result already in hand would be moving the goalposts, and was declined rather than offered as a route to a green report |
| D-325 | **The remaining work is ordered cheapest first: the external-clock guard on the ACC-7 and SUGR paths, then D-274's SUGR job on TK5, then the `right` variant probe.** Each lands before the next begins | Start the `right` probe first and write the guard while it computes, as earlier today; the SUGR job alone; the `right` probe alone | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. Serialising also keeps each result interpretable: the SUGR job is a TK5 run whose step times are reported, and the `right` probe is 16 x86 workers — overlapping them is what stretched ONFPRUN from 63 minutes to 2 h 41 min under D-304 |
| D-326 | **The `right` variant probe runs at 30 seeds**, ACC-4's own seed count | Four seeds first as a screen, the variant being very unlikely to have a feasible window; do not run it at all, since D-177 already declined to adopt the `right` set | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, following D-309's precedent. A four-seed pass already produced a **wrong mechanism** once — VL-100 read the 40 Hz column as non-monotonic and insensitive to W_syn, and VL-103 at thirty seeds showed it cleanly monotonic — so the cheap screen was declined for the same reason it was declined then |
| D-327 | **The progress CLI covers MVS jobs on TK5 as well as x86 ones, so every run in flight is visible the same way — including the one already running when this was asked for.** Instructed directly by the owner: *"can we also make sure we can check progress the same way for all the runs that we are going to run and running at the moment"* | Leave MVS jobs invisible, the x86 side being covered; wait and add it when the next MVS job is launched | **The x86 technique does not transfer, and that is the point.** `tools/progress.py` watches worker PROCESSES; an MVS job has none on the host — `tools/mvsrun.py` pushes a card deck through the reader and then polls, so from outside Hercules there is one idle python process and nothing else. What MVS does expose is its console log, where every step completion is one IEFACTRT line carrying the step name, the program and the return code. **That is a better signal than the x86 side has**, because it reports outcomes rather than activity: `ok SCRATCH IEFBR14 COND CODE 0000`. **One defect fixed on sight:** `render_brief` returned a `no jobs running` placeholder of its own, which printed directly above a TK5 job that was plainly running. Only the caller sees every source, so only the caller may decide there is nothing |
| D-328 | **The MVS tools open a progress window too, so every long job does it, not just the x86 ones.** `tools/mvsrun.py` on `--req`, `--run`, `--buzz` and the three installs, and `tools/mvsjcc.py` on `--run`; `--no-window` suppresses it and `--print` never triggers it. Instructed directly by the owner: *"can you open the terminal for the job and make sure in other jobs the terminal opens as well"* | Leave the MVS tools alone, the owner opening a window by hand when they launch one; open a window for every invocation of those tools | **Only the paths that SUBMIT are covered, and that distinction is the whole design.** `--cards`, `--compare`, `--recover`, `--report` and `--print` are local operations over files that finish in seconds; a window for one of those would open, report `no jobs running`, and then sit there — training the owner to ignore a window that is supposed to mean something. `mvsjcc`'s probes are excluded for the same reason: `--ccprobe` is about a minute of TK5 and the window would outlive what it watches. D-323 did the same for the x86 tools |
| D-329 | **An MVS job's progress view shows a liveness signal of its own — the share of a host core Hercules is using — and the x86 run log is hidden when there is no x86 work.** Raised by the owner pasting their window, which showed *"(no run has started or finished since watching began)"* under a job that was using 100% of a core | Leave the section and explain that it is x86-only; drop the run log entirely | **The section was empty BY CONSTRUCTION, which is the worst kind of empty.** It is built by diffing `runnet` worker processes and an MVS job has none, so on a TK5-only screen it could never say anything else — and it reads as nothing happening. **The deeper problem it exposed:** an MVS job has no equivalent of sixteen workers churning. STEP2 can run forty minutes without MVS printing a line, so a step list that has not changed looks exactly like a job that has died. Hercules' host CPU separates them: about one core means the simulation is running. It is the same clock `mvsprf.trust()` certifies ACC-6 on, used here only to answer *is it doing anything*, never to time anything. Sampled across refreshes, so in a watch loop it costs nothing |
| D-330 | **Both FR-BAT-04 defects the SUGR job exposed are fixed, and SUGR is re-run so the requirement is evidenced rather than asserted:** the report's rate field gains a signed picture, and the `path` names file is installed before the job | Fix both and verify off-MVS through the GnuCOBOL proxy, saving about 75 minutes of TK5 but leaving FR-BAT-04 fixed-but-unproven on the real platform; fix the sign only, treating the names gap as operational; record both as findings and change nothing | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. **Neither defect could have been found any other way:** D-274's SUGR job is the only one that drives the `path` suite through ONFLYDRV, and it had never been run. **(1) The names.** Every readout printed `*UNNAMED*`. MVS holds one names dataset, `HERC01.ONFLY.ONFNAM`, and the installed copy is `srext`'s, whose readout ids are 00009 and 00088; `path`'s are 00013 and 00302. The names exist in `data/networks/onfnam-malecns-v1.0-path.txt` and nothing installs them, so FR-BAT-04's *"each readout neuron's name"* is unmet for that network. **(2) The sign.** G-13 is rate **−1** and the report printed `RATE= 1`. `RL-RATE` is `PIC ZZZ9`, unsigned, receiving the signed `ONF-STIM-RATE`, and COBOL drops the sign on that MOVE. **Display only** — the fingerprint `A30B1F03` matches Section 8.4, so the engine received −1 correctly. The fix goes in `cobol/ONFLYDRV.cbl`, **never** in `generated/ONFLYDRV.cbl**, which the generator owns |
| D-331 | **The SUGR re-run is queued behind the `right` variant probe rather than run alongside it**, keeping D-325's serialisation | Run it now alongside the probe, SUGR's verdict being correctness rather than timing and the D-329 clock guard being able to state plainly that the times are not clean; stop the probe, run SUGR, restart the probe from zero | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. The probe was nine minutes into a 4.6-hour run when the question arose. The 2026-09-15 precedent is the reason it was asked rather than assumed: ONFPRUN stretched from 63 minutes to 2 h 41 min sharing a host under D-304 |
| D-332 | **Each demonstration job carries its own network's names inline in the deck, instead of every job reading the single installed `HERC01.ONFLY.ONFNAM`.** BUZZ carries `srext`'s sixteen rows, SUGR carries `path`'s | Install the right names per network before each run, as D-330 had said; keep the DSN wiring but have `mvsrun` refuse to submit when the installed names do not cover the network's readout indices; two datasets, one per network | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion after the engineer measured the two facts that decide it. **(1) The files are sixteen rows**, not hundreds, so inlining costs 16 cards against SUGR's ~2,148-card network deck — the size objection does not exist. **(2) Today a mismatch is loud only by luck.** The names file maps INDEX → name and the index is a position in one network's neuron array, so it is network-specific by construction; `srext`'s indices are `00009 00088 00128 …` and `path`'s are `00013 00302 00567 …`, and **zero appear in both**, which is why the wrong file gave `*UNNAMED*` rather than a wrong name. Nothing guarantees that: `path` has a row at `00013`, and an extraction putting an `srext` readout at index 13 would print a real, plausible neuron name against the wrong neuron with no check anywhere noticing. **A wrong name in a demonstration report is worse than a missing one**, because a reviewer cannot tell. **No requirement text changes:** IR-JCL-01's table fixes ONFNAM's attributes (FB, LRECL=80) and contents, not whether the DD is a DSN or an instream, and a `DD *` stream is FB/80 by definition. ONFERPT has used an inline ONFNAM all along, so this makes the demonstration jobs agree with the report job rather than differ from it |
| D-333 | **The TK5 console is revived without restarting Hercules, if it can be.** Diagnose first — whether the HTTP port was dropped from the running configuration and whether it can be re-enabled from the emulator itself | Restart Hercules and re-IPL TK5, then reset the codepage (a restart silently reverts it, VL-18); leave TK5 down and record that the SUGR re-run could not be made | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. **The failure is partial, which is why it was worth asking:** Hercules is alive (PID 3572, 16,188 s of CPU) and ports 3270 and 3505 still listen, but **8038 does not**. `tools/mvsub.py` checks the 819/1047 codepage through that console before every submission (D-101, VL-18), so with it gone no job can be submitted at all — `install-drv` failed with `WrongCodepage: cannot reach the Hercules console`. **The guard in the re-run script did its job:** it refused to run SUGR against a load module that had not been rebuilt, which would have printed the old unsigned rate and proved nothing while looking like proof. MVS has been up since 13:33 the previous day, so a restart is not free |
| D-334 | **The owner types `http start` in the Hercules window to revive the console; the engineer waits, verifies port 8038 and the 819/1047 codepage, then runs `install-drv` and the SUGR re-run.** No restart, no re-IPL | Restart Hercules and re-IPL TK5, which is fully automatic from the engineer's side but costs the codepage and an uptime since the previous day; leave TK5 down and close out with FR-BAT-04 verified only by the GnuCOBOL proxy and the deck tests | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. **The only programmatic command path into Hercules is the HTTP console on 8038 itself**, which is the thing that died — `CNSLPORT 3270` is the terminal port for VTAM sessions, not a command channel — so the one non-destructive route is a command typed at the emulator's own window, which the engineer cannot do |
| D-335 | **`tools/progress.py` consolidates its four separate PowerShell queries into one CIM call per refresh** | Also raise the watcher's default interval from 15 s to 60 s for MVS-only jobs; change nothing, the probe's own churn being the larger source | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion after the engineer reported a possible contribution by their own tooling. **The Hercules HTTP server died at 12:40:04 with `HHC01800E ... select(): the system lacked sufficient buffer space or ... a queue was full`** — WSAENOBUFS — as the `right` probe was starting 16 continuously-recycled worker processes. `progress.py` was spawning a **separate PowerShell subprocess per data source per refresh** — `_workers()`, `_running_jobs()`, `_worker_count()` and `herc_rate()` — up to four every 15 seconds **per open window**, with two or three windows open. Causation is not proven and is not claimed; the churn is real, it is the engineer's own code, and it is unnecessary: one `Get-CimInstance Win32_Process` returns the command lines, the creation times **and** `KernelModeTime`/`UserModeTime`, so every consumer can be fed from a single snapshot |
| D-336 | **Hercules is restarted WITHOUT `-d`, by writing `CONSOLE` into `unattended/mode`, so TK5 comes up with a real console window and a future HTTP-server death is recoverable by a typed command rather than another restart.** Supersedes D-334, whose premise turned out to be false | Restart exactly as configured, keeping daemon mode, which changes least but leaves the same trap; leave TK5 down and close out with FR-BAT-04 proven only off-MVS | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion **after the engineer corrected their own instruction.** D-334 told the owner to type `http start` in the Hercules window. **There is no such window.** `mvs.bat` sets `SET DAEMON=-d` and launches `hercules.exe -d -f conf	k5.cnf`; `-d` is daemon mode — no panel, no keyboard input — and PID 3572 has `MainWindowHandle: 0` and an empty title, confirming it at the process level. The script drops `-d` only when `unattended/mode` contains `CONSOLE`. So a daemon-mode Hercules has exactly **one** command interface, the HTTP server on 8038, and when that thread died the lab became unreachable with no way back but a restart. **This is a lab configuration change**, which is why it was asked |
| D-337 | **Hercules is killed rather than quiesced, because MVS cannot be quiesced: `scripts/shutdown` is a Hercules `hao` script invoked through the console, and the console is what died.** The 3270 port reaches VTAM and TSO, not the MVS master console | Attempt a quiesce through a job submitted on the working card reader, MVS 3.8j JCL having a `COMMAND` statement; leave Hercules running with a dead console and do no more MVS work this session | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion, with the risk stated rather than waved away. The 2026-09-15 handover's warning — *"a shutdown around an active initiator is how a 1981 operating system ends up with datasets it never closed"* — was about an **active initiator**, and there is none: SUGR completed and was purged, and the install failed before it submitted anything, so JES2 is idle. What is at risk is only whatever MVS has buffered for catalogs and spool. Quiescing JES2 from a job running under JES2 is in any case self-defeating |
| D-338 | **The report's rate picture becomes a floating sign, `PIC ----9`, and is proved on MVS** | Leave `PIC -ZZZ9`, which is correct COBOL, shows the sign and is already evidenced on the real platform by VL-110; change it but verify only through the GnuCOBOL proxy, leaving VL-110's MVS evidence describing a superseded picture | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. `PIC -ZZZ9` is a **fixed** insertion sign: it occupies column one while `ZZZ9` right-justifies the digit, so −1 renders `-   1`. A floating `PIC ----9` renders `   -1` at the same five columns, and `-9999` / ` 9999` at the extremes. The owner chose to keep shipped COBOL and MVS evidence in step rather than let them diverge, at a cost of one install and one ~75-minute SUGR run |
| D-339 | **The session closes on the final test run, `git diff --stat` and the goal report**, with ACC-4 reported as the evidenced finding it is | Shut both labs down first, as D-293 did, then report; keep working on what remains — ACC-5 row 8 needs z/OS, Phase G is unstarted, and FR-SIM-10's streaming path has no engine implementation | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. Every Phase E exit criterion is now measured in this session. **The labs are therefore left running**, which the next session must know: D-293's precedent is that they are shut down cleanly before the owner travels with the machine, and TK5 now runs in CONSOLE mode (D-336) rather than as a daemon |
| D-340 | **ACC-4 is amended: the shape clause becomes the criterion, and the magnitude comparison becomes a reported quantity rather than a pass/fail test.** The exact wording is drafted as P-16 for the owner's approval before it enters Section 6.4 | Accept the goal's PARTIAL outcome and carry ACC-4 as a failing criterion under D-310; amend ACC-4 by excluding rates whose reference is near zero, the narrower D-172/D-202 precedent — **which does not work**, since it excuses 10 Hz (reference exactly 0.00) but not 40 Hz (reference 4.73 against ONFLY's 14.35), leaving ACC-4 still failing; keep working on something else | Chosen by the owner through AskUserQuestion. **This moves the bar and the record says so.** The grounds are VL-06, already in this document: *"ACC-4 compares a male connectome with a model calibrated on a female connectome. Agreement shows consistency, not identity."* The evidence is VL-103, VL-108 and VL-109 — **no W_syn satisfies the magnitude clause for any stimulus set ever considered**, the shipped D-52 set included, because the dose-response curve differs from Shiu's in *shape* and W_syn is a single scalar. The shape clause, meanwhile, **passes on measurement**: `ACC-4 shape PASS (onset onfly 10 Hz, reference 40 Hz: PASS)`.
